      validation slot when its only peer-reviewed support is on a different instrument in a different regime? **Eleventh-cycle update: the question that was recorded as binding at the end of the tenth cycle is now substantially answered, and the binding question has moved.** The tenth cycle's binding question was whether the free open-interest-clearing proxy is a faithful stand-in for a liquidation event. It is now answerable, and partially answered: the proxy is a state and the payoff is a flow; the published October 2025 magnitudes pair the two on the same window (open interest clearing 25-70% against $0.96bn of book-hitting forced sales, $733m of it after onset); and two free forced-liquidation records now exist against which the proxy can be validated directly - the Hyperliquid on-chain forced-fill record (per contract, per fill, with direction, 2025-03-27 to 2026-06-30) and the Zenodo daily eleven-venue panel (365 days). The eleventh cycle also found that the proxy as written in v1.5 was specified on the USD-notional series and would have registered a price decline as a liquidation event; that defect is fixed in v1.6. The binding question for the eleventh and twelfth cycles is therefore no longer "is the proxy faithful" but two sharper things. (i) *On the entry's own target instrument, is there any free forced-liquidation record at all?* The two new records are on Hyperliquid (on-chain) and cross-venue-daily (Zenodo); neither is Binance, which is the entry's specified instrument, and Binance's REST API returns HTTP 451 from this environment. The highest-value external question is now: does any free or low-cost source publish Binance USDⓈ-M per-contract long-versus-short liquidation notionals on a multi-year window, and if not, can the proxy's fidelity be established on Hyperliquid and then transported to Binance only with an explicit, pre-registered transport assumption? The transport assumption is the thing that would have to be argued, and it is the same shape of problem as the entry's instrument-specificity problem (dated futures versus perpetuals) in a new place. (ii) *The identity of the forced-unwinder is now documented and it changes who bears the cost.* If front-end traders are 96% of liquidation volume and the venue's own settlement rules suspend the insurance fund in the final hour, then the entry's crash-conditionality claim is a claim about retail crowding, and a validator should report the trigger state separately for front-end and non-front-end positioning where the venue permits. A third, smaller question is whether the SaR framework (arXiv:2603.09164) offers a forward-looking substitute for the proxy that does not require post-hoc clearing to be detected, and whether it is computable on free data for Binance; if it is, it would replace the retrospective proxy with a prospective one and would materially strengthen the entry's design.
**Thirteenth-cycle update: the binding question has changed again, and it is now a window question rather than an access question.** The twelfth cycle's binding question was whether any free source publishes Binance USDⓈ-M per-contract long-versus-short liquidation notionals on a multi-year window. It is now answered, and the answer has two parts. (i) **For USDⓈ-M — the entry's specified instrument — the answer is no**, established by exhaustive enumeration of the bucket: there is no liquidation dataset in the UM tree, the UM REST API is HTTP 451 from this environment, and the two free forced-liquidation records that do exist (the Hyperliquid on-chain fill log and the Zenodo daily eleven-venue panel) are on other venues. (ii) **For Binance itself — on the inverse family — the answer is yes, and it is complete for the registered statistic**: a per-contract, per-event, side-attributed record (2023-06-25 to 2024-10-14, 17,182 symbol-days) with a matching contract-count open-interest series, from which the registered P1 statistic is computable in a single dimensionless unit.
The binding question is therefore no longer *whether the statistic is computable* but **whether the measurement transfers from the instrument where it is computable to the instrument the entry specifies, and whether the 2024-10-14 end of the publisher window is a data boundary or an artefact of the publisher's own choices.** Three sub-questions follow, in order. (a) *Transport.* The COIN-M perps and the UM perps on the same asset are separate contracts with separate open-interest pools, separate funding mechanics and separate participants, so a measurement on BTCUSD_PERP is a *replication* of a measurement on BTCUSDT, not the same measurement; the transport assumption would have to be stated, and its falsification criterion would have to be pre-committed (the natural candidate is the ratio of the two contracts' open interest, which the archive does not publish for a given date, so the assumption may be untestable and therefore inadmissible). (b) *Window.* The publisher window ends 2024-10-14, and no later Binance-native forced-liquidation record is known; if the entry's critical regime is the post-July-2024 window, then the directly measurable arm covers roughly three months of it, and the remainder of the window is reachable only through the proxy. (c) *Interpretation.* The cycle's own single-day measurement shows the proxy registering a state change 10-14x larger than the forced flow that accounts for it; if that ratio is stable, then any published or preregistered result that used an open-interest clearing proxy as a stand-in for a liquidation event is measuring a different quantity from the one it claims to measure, and the entry's mandatory proxy-fidelity step is the place where that has to be settled. **Fourteenth-cycle in-place correction: "10-14x" is wrong (the true ratios are 6.9x and 9.9x) and the conditional is now answered in the negative — the ratio is *not* stable.** Across the 36 contracts that have a file on 2024-08-05 the open-interest-reduction-to-forced-flow ratio runs from **0.56 to 34.2**, and for BTCUSD_PERP alone it runs 8.2 (2024-08-04), 6.9 (2024-08-05) and 4.3 (2024-08-06). The ratio therefore cannot be a fixed constant of the market or of the instrument, and any result that used an open-interest clearing proxy as a stand-in for a liquidation event is measuring a quantity whose relationship to the flow varies by two orders of magnitude across contracts on the same day.

**Fourteenth-cycle update: the binding question is no longer a question about the record's end; it is a question about retrieval, and the record itself has been shown to need a stated missingness treatment.** The thirteenth cycle left three sub-questions open: (a) *transport* — whether a measurement on the inverse family transports to the entry's specified linear family; (b) *window* — what the 2024-10-14 end means; (c) *interpretation* — whether the proxy-to-flow ratio is stable. **All three are now answered, and the answers re-point the entry.** (c) is answered in the negative and with a magnitude: the ratio is not stable, running from 0.56x to 34.2x across contracts on one day, so the open-interest-clearing proxy cannot be treated as a flow measure at any fixed conversion. (b) is answered by a primary venue document: the product was **discontinued by the publisher on 2024-10-15**, so the window's end is a publisher decision, and — the more consequential half — the **USDⓈ-M record for the entry's specified instrument existed from 2023-06-25 to 2024-03-31 and has been removed from the archive**, so the entry's own instrument carried the statistic for 281 days and the archive no longer serves it. (a) therefore changes character: it is no longer a question about transporting a measurement across instrument families, because **the entry's own instrument can be measured directly if the withdrawn files can be retrieved**; the question becomes a retrieval question, and the natural next step is a search for a mirror or an archive of the withdrawn UM files (the publisher's own 2025 request for restoration is a dated, citable statement that the files existed and were withdrawn). The remaining sub-question is therefore the sharpest one the entry has faced: **does any copy of the withdrawn USDⓈ-M `liquidationSnapshot` files exist outside the publisher's bucket, and if so does it cover the post-July-2024 regime** — because the UM record's documented window ends 2024-03-31, so a mirror of exactly the withdrawn files would close the entry's *construction* question but **not** its *regime* question, and the entry's critical window would still be reachable only through the now-known-unstable proxy or through the COIN-M replication. A fourth question is new and is the one the next cycle should take up: **given that the record's file set is a 64%-complete panel with two distinct absence regimes, what declaration of missingness makes the registered statistic's cross-sectional form well-posed**, and does the 2024-08-05 cross-section (36 of 45 contracts, a statistic spanning 0.26% to 37.94% of opening open interest) survive a notional-weighted aggregation and an equal-weighted one simultaneously?

## CRYPTO-EDGE-002 — Funding Extreme Contrarian (Crowded Leverage Unwind), v1.5

1. Short name and version: Funding Extreme Contrarian v1.2. **v1.3 is the thirteenth cycle's amendment**: it records three new independent 2026 sources outside the Shek programme that bear on the entry's registered claim, none of which reproduces the conditional design and all of which disagree with one another; it supersedes in place the eleventh cycle's record that no independent test of the funding-extreme claim exists beyond the failed preregistered test; and it records that the rejection is **corroborated** rather than disturbed. **No dimension moves and the total stays at 46.** **v1.4 is the fourteenth cycle's amendment**: it sharpens the entry's **reinstatement condition** rather than its claim, on the strength of a new access finding — the venue's own per-contract, per-event forced-liquidation record **existed on this entry's specified instrument (USDⓈ-M) from 2023-06-25 to 2024-03-31 and has been removed from the archive**, while the inverse-family equivalent survives for 2023-06-25 to 2024-10-14 as a 64%-complete panel — so a future reinstatement attempt can, for the first time, verify the entry's own *mechanism* (crowding to forced unwind) on a per-event basis in the same window as the claim, instead of inferring it from an open-interest state change whose ratio to the flow is now measured to vary from 0.56x to 34.2x across contracts; it records the **fourth silent-failure defect in that record** (an absent file is not automatically a zero-event day) and the two distinct absence regimes; and it records the **correction of the "10-14x" proxy-to-flow ratio** the catalogue carried, which is 6.9x and 9.9x. **No dimension moves and the total stays at 46**: the cycle's findings bear on the entry's *mechanism's observability* and on the terms under which it could be reopened, not on the registered prediction, whose rejection stands.
2. One-sentence thesis: Extreme funding-rate readings (top/bottom decile of the trailing distribution) signal overcrowded leveraged positioning prone to forced unwind, predicting short-horizon mean reversion opposite to the funding extreme.
3. Status: **rejected** (was shortlisted at rank 3 until the sixth research cycle). Removed from the ranked shortlist; the reason is recorded in REJECTED_AND_DEFERRED.md so the idea is not rediscovered. Reinstated only if a material new fact appears, per item 17.
4. Proposed edge family and target market/universe: Derivatives positioning/flow anomaly (crowded leverage, forced liquidation feedback); BTC and ETH perpetuals on retail-accessible venues with public funding history.
5. Mechanism: Positive funding means longs pay shorts - crowded longs. When sentiment turns, leveraged longs hit maintenance margin; the exchange emits forced, price-insensitive market sells, pushing price further down into the next thresholds - the leverage-manufactured feedback documented in the liquidation-cascade literature (Garcia Seuma 2026; BitMEX study showing ~60X average leverage among the liquidated and substantial daily liquidation %). Crowding persists because momentum chasers enter late and leverage is sticky. Mirror image for extreme negative funding (crowded shorts, short-squeeze exposure). Structural amendment (v1.1): the funding rate is by construction proportional to the futures-spot gap, so a gap driven by momentum / positive feedback generates the same funding extremes as a gap driven by crowding; the mechanism therefore does not by itself distinguish this hypothesis from a momentum-reversal rule, and the perp-era compression of dislocations means extreme funding states are rarer and less extreme than in the pre-2022 sample. Structural amendment (v1.2): the market-structure description is retained, but the inference from crowding to imminent reversal is withdrawn, because three preregistered tests in a public ledger now show that in crypto a crowding-style extreme is followed by *continuation* rather than exhaustion (see items 6, 13 and 15) — funding extremes, elevated perp basis and cross-venue premiums all failed to reverse and all leaned the wrong way. The forced-liquidation feedback loop itself is unaffected and remains a published finding; what the evidence rejects is the timing claim that an extreme is followed by an unwind. This distinction is the reason the entry is rejected as a *return* hypothesis rather than as a *mechanism*.
6. Exact falsifiable prediction: Funding rate in the top decile of the trailing 90-day distribution predicts negative returns over the next 1-7 days; bottom decile predicts positive returns. Direction: opposite to funding sign. Horizon: 1-7 days. Addendum (v1.1): the prediction must hold incrementally after controlling for CRYPTO-EDGE-001 basis and for trailing returns; if the decile signal adds nothing beyond those, it is not a distinct edge. **Outcome (v1.2): the registered prediction was tested and failed on both preregistered criteria, with point estimates of the wrong sign.** The test used the same signal construction as this entry (trailing-90-day percentile rank of daily funding, LOW <= 10th, HIGH >= 90th, fixed in advance), on Binance USDT-margined perpetuals joined to spot, across 8 symbols and 17,910 symbol-days from December 2019 to July 2026 — a window that covers the post-July-2024 period this entry's own open question had identified as untested. The 7-day LOW-minus-HIGH difference was -0.95% with a 95% moving-block bootstrap CI of [-3.20%, +1.39%], and the sign was positive in only 4 of 8 symbols against a preregistered requirement of 5. Both point-estimate directions leaned the wrong way: HIGH-funding days were followed by +3.30% versus +2.35% at 7 days and +12.61% versus +4.83% at 30 days. A momentum-flavored variant (HIGH funding read as confirmation rather than exhaustion) was tested in the same ledger and also failed to clear its bar at 30 days (+7.78%, CI [-0.77%, +17.58%]), so the opposite sign was *not* promoted either. Both directions of the funding-extreme claim are therefore closed on the available evidence.
7. Candidate explanatory variables and proposed signal concept: Signal = 8-hour funding rate from public exchange data, expressed as a percentile of the trailing 90-day distribution computed using only past data (no look-ahead); signal observed at a funding timestamp, forward window starts after. Plain language: "are traders paying an extreme premium to hold one side?"
8. Expected trade expression: Contrarian - short when funding is extremely positive, long when extremely negative; shorting feasibility is a separate question (borrow, exchange constraints). Not market-neutral; avoid strong trending news regimes as a filter. Alignment note: when contrarian direction coincides with receiving funding, the carry helps rather than hurts.
9. Primary comparator or null explanation: Random entry with the same hold period; spot buy-and-hold; funding series time-shuffled as a placebo; CRYPTO-EDGE-001 (basis level) as an alternative positioning proxy to test whether funding adds incremental information; momentum-matched entry (v1.1, mandatory) given that the funding rate is proportional to the futures-spot gap.
10. Data required: 8-hour funding rates, mark price, spot price; open interest as context; history at least 2 years; free public endpoints verified 2026-10-20 - Binance /fapi/v1/fundingRate, Bybit /v5/market/funding/history, OKX /api/v5/public/funding-rate-history, Binance public data archive for klines. Access classification: 1 (confirmed free, public) for funding and price on major venues; 2 (granularity/history limits unverified) for open interest and liquidation detail. **Fourteenth-cycle refinement of the last clause, marked superseded in place: the "liquidation detail" data class is access 1, not access 2, and it is access 1 in two senses.** (i) On Binance's **inverse** family a free, dated, per-contract, per-event, side-attributed forced-liquidation record exists for **2023-06-25 to 2024-10-14** (`data/futures/cm/daily/liquidationSnapshot/`), with the caveats the catalogue now carries in full: a 64%-complete panel in which an absent file is not automatically a zero-event day, exact-two row duplication, a per-symbol contract multiplier, and order-side semantics. (ii) On this entry's **specified** instrument, the USDⓈ-M family, the same product **existed from 2023-06-25 to 2024-03-31 and has been removed from the archive** — the venue's own issue tracker records both the removal and the request for its restoration — so the class was access 1 on the entry's own instrument for 281 days and its current absence is a **withdrawal**, not a non-existence. The "2 (granularity/history limits unverified)" classification of liquidation detail is therefore **superseded in place**.
11. Plausible friction and capacity considerations: funding receipt is favorable in the contrarian direction when sign-aligned, but adverse when it is not; slippage and whipsaws in strong trends can dominate; exchange-specific funding formula and clip; exchange leverage rules affect how quickly unwinds occur. No measured friction values.
12. Why meaningfully different from the catalogue and prior art: Mechanism is funding-rate flow extremes and forced-unwind feedback, distinct from the carry level in CRYPTO-EDGE-001 and from the cross-venue relative measure in CRYPTO-EDGE-003. Distinct from generic price oscillators (e.g. RSI overbought) because it uses derivatives positioning, not price history alone - but note (v1.1) that the informational content of the funding extreme overlaps materially with momentum.
13. Strongest argument against; likely failure regimes; confounders: (i) Extremes can persist for extended trending periods (funding can stay positive for months in a bull run). (ii) Timing of the unwind is unpredictable; false positives are frequent. (iii) Little rigorous published evidence ties funding percentiles specifically to forward returns - most sources are practitioner/anecdotal. (iv) He et al. (2026) show the futures-spot gap is largely explained by past returns, so a funding extreme is largely a momentum extreme; the contrarian rule may be a momentum-reversal rule wearing a derivatives label. (v) Zhivkov (2026) reports funding rates clustering near zero across venues (interquartile range 1.20 bps for centralized venues), so genuine extremes are rare events and any percentile threshold will be estimated on a thin tail. (vi) Gornall, Rinaldi & Xiao (2025) report that perpetuals reduce extreme price dislocations, which mechanically shrinks the trigger population in the perp era. (vii) Garcia Seuma (2026) shows liquidation timing is not predictable from a stable precursor, and that a news-driven cascade shows no precursor at all. (viii, added in v1.2 — decisive) The mechanism's central premise is empirically backwards in crypto: crowding does not mark exhaustion, it accompanies continuation. The preregistered test of this entry's registered prediction failed on both of its criteria with wrong-signed point estimates (item 6), and the same ledger's sibling test of the perp-basis level failed in the same direction (CRYPTO-EDGE-001, item 15), and its cross-venue continuation test also failed in the continuation direction. Three preregistered tests in one public ledger, covering funding extremes, perp basis levels and cross-venue premiums, all found that the crowding signal predicts *more* of the same rather than a reversal — a consistent pattern that is far more informative than any single failed test. The mechanism can still be true about *forced liquidation mechanics* (Garcia Seuma 2026; BitMEX 2021); what fails is the inference from crowding to imminent reversal. Likely failure regimes: strong momentum regimes, exogenous news shocks, and — for this entry specifically — every regime, since the sign of the point estimates was wrong rather than merely imprecise.
14. Research-only validation plan to be executed elsewhere: **superseded in v1.2.** The plan of record for this entry was a preregistered decile test of funding extremes against forward spot returns, and that test has now been run by an independent party and failed on both preregistered criteria (item 6, and item 15 for the source). No further validation of this entry is authorised in this repository. Closure conditions, all of which would have to be met before the entry could be reopened: (a) an independent preregistered test, on a sample disjoint from the failed one and with a window extending beyond July 2026, in which the sign of the LOW-minus-HIGH forward-return difference is positive with a bootstrap interval excluding zero, and in which the sign is positive for at least five of eight symbols; or (b) a published peer-reviewed study establishing that funding extremes forecast forward returns in the contrarian direction with a mechanism that is not simply momentum reversal; or (c) a documented structural change in the funding mechanism (for example a regime in which funding is no longer proportional to the futures-spot gap, or in which venue leverage limits are relaxed enough to permit crowded positioning to build) that would invalidate the failed test's mapping. Failing all three, the entry stays rejected and must not be re-listed under a new name, window, venue or threshold.
15. Evidence notes with sources and dates: Garcia Seuma (2026) arXiv:2607.27070 (v1, 30 Jul 2026) and arXiv:2608.03616, both free - forced-liquidation feedback and cascade mechanics; the newer reading adds that pre-crash signatures are event-heterogeneous, that the October 2025 in-sample pattern inverts on the August 2024 cascade, and that the February 2025 news shock shows no precursor (checked 2026-10-20). arXiv:2102.04591 BitMEX leverage/liquidation study, free - substantial forced liquidations, ~60X average leverage among liquidated; single-venue sample ending Feb 2021. He, Manela, Ross & von Wachter (2026) arXiv:2212.06888v7 - funding is proportional to the futures-spot gap; deviations diminish over time; momentum explains much of the gap (checked 2026-10-20). Zhivkov (2026), "The Two-Tiered Structure of Cryptocurrency Funding Rate Markets", Mathematics 14(2):346, doi 10.3390/math14020346, open access - funding clusters near zero (IQR 1.20 bps CEX); frictions manifest in cross-platform spreads rather than average levels (checked 2026-10-20). Gornall, Rinaldi & Xiao (2025) SSRN 5036933 - perps reduce extreme price dislocations. Gate.io Wiki funding/OI/liquidation article - free but exchange-published and marketing-adjacent - treated as anecdotal, not evidence. **Added in v1.2 (checked 2026-10-20):** martex-quant public research ledger, "Hypothesis 08 — Funding-Rate Extremes as a Contrarian Signal" (docs/hypotheses/08-funding-extremes.md, verdict 2026-07-11), free GitHub raw, access 1 for the document; a preregistered kill test with the prediction, the data source, the fixed trailing-90-day percentile window and the 10th/90th-percentile thresholds, the primary metric (pooled E[fwd 7d | LOW] − E[fwd 7d | HIGH]), and two explicit pass bars (interval excluding zero, and sign positive for at least 5 of 8 symbols), all fixed before the run; result FAIL on both bars, 17,910 symbol-days, 2019-12 to 2026-07, 7-day LOW-minus-HIGH = -0.95% with CI [-3.20%, +1.39%], sign consistency 4/8, HIGH-funding days followed by higher returns at 7 and 30 days; the momentum-flavored variant (HIGH funding as confirmation) was also tested and not promoted because its 30-day point estimate +7.78% had CI [-0.77%, +17.58%]. Establishes: a direct, dated, preregistered, publicly auditable test of this entry's core claim on retail perpetuals, covering the previously untested post-July-2024 window, with wrong-signed point estimates; does not establish: that the mechanism of forced liquidation is false, that funding is uninformative about volatility (it is documented to predict volatility — Journal of Financial Markets, 2026, access 2), or that no other conditioning of funding could work. Labels: the *supporting* evidence for this entry's registered prediction is now empty — there is no published study and the one preregistered test contradicts it; the forced-liquidation mechanism remains a published finding; the decile thresholds, the trailing window and any incremental-power claim remain assumptions.
**Thirteenth-cycle sources (2026-10-20), all outside the Shek programme, and all bearing on the entry's *registered* claim without reproducing its conditional design.**
(a) **Memon, Anklesaria-Dalal and Mishra (2026), "Extreme Perpetual Futures Funding and Subsequent Bitcoin Returns: Intraday Evidence from Binance", AIJMR 4(4), 29 August 2026, doi 10.62127/aijmr.2026.v04i04.1493** — access 1 for the article page and abstract; a journal article whose peer-review standing is **not established**, so it is treated as dated public evidence and not as a peer-reviewed replication. *Establishes*, on Binance BTCUSDT funding from 10 September 2019 to 10 August 2026 against 15-minute spot prices, with extreme funding classified ex ante by the **mid-rank percentile within the preceding 180-day distribution** and the lower and upper deciles as the extreme states, that **extreme negative funding is followed by an average 24-hour Bitcoin return of +0.506%**, significant under t, Wilcoxon and directional-frequency tests and robust to false-discovery-rate adjustment, and approximately +0.491 percentage points in categorical HAC regressions controlling for the pre-settlement return, with non-overlapping 24-hour subsamples retaining positive significant means; that **extreme positive funding shows no comparable 24-hour effect**; and that the evidence suggests funding information is nonlinear, asymmetric and horizon dependent. *Does not establish* the entry's registered prediction: the test is **unconditioned on market direction**, which is the variable Shek's design turns on; it applies no realised-volatility control; its event definition is a 180-day mid-rank decile rather than Shek's; and it is a single asset on a single venue. *Bears on* the entry as an independent, dated, adjacent result that runs **against** the twelfth cycle's recorded direction of the nearest adjacent test on the negative tail, which was the "extreme up shocks continue" result: a negative tail that reverts is the *contrarian* sign that this rejected entry asserted and that the failed preregistered test contradicted.
(b) **MarketTrace (2026-07-13), "What happens after extreme funding rates? 789 days of data"** (https://markettrace.ai/blog/funding-rate-extremes) — access 1 for the article; a practitioner source with a stated method, so it sits below the entry's original preregistered test in evidential weight and is labelled as such. *Establishes*, on 789 days (15 May 2024 to 13 July 2026) across BTC, ETH, SOL, BNB, XRP and DOGE and three venues, with percentile-ranked funding computed as-of each hour, non-overlapping 72-hour episodes (BTC's 1,136 top-decile hours collapsing to 37 episodes) and medians rather than means, that BTC's top decile was followed by a median 72-hour return of -1.18% with a 40.5% hit rate, that the same condition on ETH, BNB, XRP and DOGE was followed by **positive** median 72-hour returns of +0.95%, +1.10%, +1.73% and +0.72%, and that the **bottom decile predicted nothing on any of the six assets** (medians within -0.48% and +0.62%, hit rates 46.6-54.1%). *Does not establish* anything about the entry's registered prediction, and its own limitations section states that the window is one market regime. *Bears on* the entry as a third independent measurement that **disagrees with (a) on the negative tail**.
(c) **OctopusTakopi, "Funding Rates as a Cross-Sectional Factor in Perpetual Futures"** (https://github.com/OctopusTakopi/funding-rate-alpha, free, undated repository, access 1) — a public research repository with a stated protocol, not a peer-reviewed publication and not a preregistered ledger, so it is treated as practitioner-grade public evidence. *Establishes* that on a survivorship-free record of 791 USDT-margined perpetuals including every delisted contract, 2.43 million funding settlements and 5-minute open-interest metrics for 421 liquid contracts, with the funding formula verified against the premium-index archives to a 0.61 basis-point median error, the **90-day z-score predicts nothing at any horizon** (the raw level predicts returns only at eight hours and shorter, and only by a small amount), that the price coefficient of funding **changes sign between a 2020-21 reversal regime and a 2023-26 continuation regime**, that open interest builds into and through high-funding settlements (which refutes a flow reading of the rate and leaves it as a measurement of the basis), and that a carry harvest collects 8.9 basis points per day over the full sample but earns two thirds of it in 2020-21 and does not clear a threshold thereafter. *Does not establish* the entry's registered prediction. *Bears on* the entry twice: it is a **direct negative on the percentile event definition** that both this entry and the Shek design use, and it is the closest published analogue to the catalogue's own point-in-time universe construction, with a survivorship-free contract list and a verified funding formula.
16. Scores, total rank, confidence, explanation: Causal mechanism 3/5 -> 12; evidence quality 1/5 -> 3; robustness 1/5 -> 3; free-data accessibility 5/5 -> 15; net value after frictions 1/5 -> 3; falsifiability 3/5 -> 6; originality 2/5 -> 4. Total 46/100, down from 58. Changes in v1.2, all forced by the failed preregistered test rather than by re-judgement of the same facts: evidence quality 2/5 -> 1/5, because the supporting evidence for the registered prediction is now empty — there was never a published study, and the one preregistered test contradicts the prediction rather than merely failing to support it; robustness 2/5 -> 1/5, because the core prediction did not survive a test on the same instrument family that also covered the previously untested window; net value after frictions 3/5 -> 1/5, because the preregistered point estimates show the trade earning in the wrong direction (high funding followed by higher returns), which is a negative expected value for the contrarian expression rather than an unmeasured one. Mechanism (3/5) and free-data accessibility (5/5) are unchanged, because the forced-liquidation mechanism itself is a documented finding and the inputs are confirmed free; the failure is in the mapping from crowding to reversal, not in the market-structure description. Confidence: the *rejection* is held with medium confidence — the sample is large, preregistered, dated, and covers the right window, but it is a single non-peer-reviewed ledger, and Grobys and Shahzad (2026) show that crypto factor statistics of this kind have power-law tails, so a single test is informative but not conclusive on its own. Rank: removed from the shortlist; it was rank 3 at 58/100 and is now rejected at 46/100. The key point for the catalogue is that this entry did not fail because of thin evidence for a plausible mechanism — it failed because the cleanest available test of its exact registered prediction was run and came back with the wrong sign.
**Thirteenth cycle: no dimension moves; the total is 46, re-derived as 12+3+3+15+3+6+4.** The three new sources are all *adjacent* to the entry's registered prediction and none reproduces its conditional design, so the entry's evidence quality (1/5) and net value (1/5) are already at their floors and cannot fall, and no source supports the registered prediction, so nothing can rise. The cycle's finding is that the entry's **rejection is corroborated rather than disturbed**: an independent, dated, seven-year, single-venue test finds the negative tail reverting at a magnitude of the same order as the Shek programme's up-day result; a second independent measurement finds the negative tail predicting nothing at all; and a third finds the percentile event definition carrying no predictive content on a survivorship-free record. Three independent measurements of the same family now disagree with one another, which is a stronger statement than the catalogue's previous record ("no independent test exists") and a weaker one than a refutation. The rejection therefore stands on firmer ground than in the sixth cycle, and the entry remains closed.

**Fourteenth cycle: no dimension moves; the total is 46, re-derived as 12+3+3+15+3+6+4.** The cycle's findings do not bear on the registered prediction at all — the preregistered test that failed it stands, and the three adjacent measurements that disagree with one another are undisturbed — so the entry's evidence quality (1/5) and net value (1/5) remain at their floors, and no source supports the claim, so nothing can rise. What the cycle changed is the **terms of a possible reinstatement**, in two specific ways that are worth recording because they are the first time this entry's *mechanism* has become measurable per event. (i) The venue's per-contract, per-event forced-liquidation record is free and dated on the inverse family and **was** free and dated on this entry's specified USDⓈ-M instrument for 2023-06-25 to 2024-03-31 before being withdrawn from the archive, so the entry's mechanism claim ("crowding produces forced unwinds") is now directly observable on a per-event basis over a window that overlaps the failed preregistered test's own sample (2019-12 to 2026-07) — which means a reinstatement attempt can no longer be forced to argue about mechanism from a proxy. (ii) The catalogue has now measured **how bad that proxy is**: the open-interest-reduction-to-forced-flow ratio runs from 0.56x to 34.2x across contracts on a single day, so a mechanism check that used the proxy would have been uninformative by construction, and the thirteenth cycle's "10-14x" figure that the record carried was an arithmetic mis-reading (the true ratios are 6.9x and 9.9x). The entry's closure conditions are therefore **extended with a fourth**, and it is a condition on method rather than on evidence: (d) any reinstatement attempt must **verify the crowding-to-unwind link on the per-event record**, in the same window and with the same contract set as the claim, and must declare its missingness treatment for the 36.0% of symbol-days that have no file; a test that establishes the claim while using an open-interest state change as the unwind measure is not admissible, because the relation between the two quantities is not stable. The three existing closure conditions (a), (b) and (c) are unchanged, and failing all four the entry stays rejected and must not be re-listed under a new name, window, venue or threshold.

**Fifteenth cycle: no dimension moves; the total is 46, re-derived as 12+3+3+15+3+6+4, and the entry remains rejected.** The cycle's findings again do not bear on the registered prediction — the preregistered test that failed it stands, and no source in the cycle supports the claim — so the entry's evidence quality (1/5) and net value (1/5) remain at their floors and nothing can rise. What the cycle changed is the **fourth reinstatement clause, which is now partly discharged**. Clause (d) required that any reinstatement attempt *verify the crowding-to-unwind link on the per-event record, in the same window and with the same contract set as the claim, and declare its missingness treatment for the symbol-days that have no file*. That record is now located on the entry's **own specified USDⓈ-M instrument**, free, dated and checksum-verifiable at `data3/liquidationSnapshot/` (311 symbols, 64,820 files, 2023-06-25 to 2024-03-31), so the clause is satisfiable on the specified family, and the missingness treatment it demands is now a small, declarable number: **2.30% of symbol-days absent on the USDⓈ-M panel** (1,528 of 66,348), against 35.97% on the inverse COIN-M family. Two further facts sharpen the clause rather than loosen it. (i) The proxy the clause forbids is now **measured to be mostly the wrong thing**: on the cascade day the recorded forced liquidations explain only 12.1% of the cross-sectional open-interest reduction, so an open-interest state change is demonstrably a poor stand-in for an unwind measure, and a reinstatement that used it would be inadmissible on the clause's own terms. (ii) The clause's requirement that the test use the *same contract set as the claim* is now satisfiable at **per-event resolution on the claim's own instrument and with no multiplier conversion**, because the USDⓈ-M `original_quantity` is in the base asset and the notional is `quantity x price`. The other three closure conditions (a), (b) and (c) are unchanged; failing all four the entry stays rejected and must not be re-listed under a new name, window, venue or threshold.
**Seventeenth cycle: no dimension moves; the total is 46, re-derived as 12+3+3+15+3+6+4, and the entry remains rejected.** The cycle's findings again do not bear on the registered prediction — the preregistered test that failed it stands, and no source in the cycle supports the claim — so the entry is neither downgraded for a failed test nor re-specified. The free-data dimension is untouched at its 5/5 ceiling; nothing can raise it, and the cycle's access work bears on no other dimension. **One substantive thing did change, and it is recorded here because the entry's *adjacent* deferral depends on it: the crowding-to-unwind link that the deferred conditional funding-extreme split requires is now fully characterised and fully specified as a test, on the entry's own instrument and window, with a declared missingness treatment.** The relocated linear record was re-verified at panel scale (**129,640 objects; 311 symbols; 281 dates; 1,528 of 66,348 symbol-days absent = 2.30%**; eleven files SHA-256-verified against the published sidecar), the **exact-two row duplication was confirmed to hold in every one of the 64,820 files** (maximum multiplicity 2, and raw rows exactly twice unique rows in every file, so the de-duplication rule is uniform rather than sampled), the **quantity field's semantics were pinned** (`original_quantity` equals `accumulated_fill_quantity` on 100.000% of 36,421 sampled unique rows, and the linear family's no-multiplier convention was confirmed by a **cross-family calibration** against the inverse family on the same underlying and date, with a median notional-share ratio of 1.44), the **missingness treatment was tested rather than asserted** (of 1,529 absent symbol-days with a comparable previous-day close, 38.33% had the contract's open interest fall by more than 1%, against 45.01% of 28,999 contract-days that do have a file, so an absent file is not a zero-event day), and the **funding leg is free and dated for the same instruments and the same window** (the publisher's monthly per-symbol funding-rate archive, which carries an explicit `funding_interval_hours` column, so the venue's mixed four- and eight-hour settlement schedules must be read from the file rather than assumed). The panel's 8,218,185 unique forced-liquidation events, 61.95% of them SELL, with 52 of 281 days carrying a cross-sectional median forced-SELL share at or above 0.5% of opening open interest, is the *outcome* distribution a crowding-to-unwind test would be run against. **The deferral itself is unchanged**, and the reason is unchanged: the reinstatement condition requires an **independent preregistered test**, which this catalogue cannot constitute, and the entry's three evidence conditions (a), (b) and (c) are untouched by anything in this cycle. What this cycle discharges is the *designability* of the fourth clause, not the clause itself.

17. Next most valuable research question: what would constitute a material new fact? Specifically, is there any documented structural change in the crypto funding mechanism after July 2026 — venue leverage-cap changes, a change in the funding formula away from proportionality to the futures-spot gap, or a new venue class (for example a materially deeper institutional or bank-issued venue) with different positioning behaviour — that would make the failed test's mapping inapplicable? In the absence of such a change, the more valuable question shifts to the adjacent one: does perp funding predict *volatility* (not returns) well enough to be tradable as a variance or tail-risk exposure, given the Journal of Financial Markets (2026) result that funding-based predictors materially improve Bitcoin spot volatility forecasts? That is a different hypothesis from this one and is not promoted here.
**Thirteenth-cycle update: the reinstatement condition now has to be stated more sharply, because the adjacent evidence has become self-contradictory rather than merely absent.** The entry's closure condition required an independent preregistered test on a disjoint sample with a positive LOW-minus-HIGH difference; the thirteenth cycle did not find one, and instead found that the *unconditioned* literature on the negative tail now points in three directions at once — a reversal of about +0.5% at 24 hours on one venue and window, no effect on six assets over 789 days, and no content at all in the percentile statistic on a survivorship-free record. A reinstatement attempt must therefore specify, in advance, which of these three results it is trying to overturn and why, and must pre-commit the direction-conditioning rule and the realised-volatility control; and it must confront the possibility that the negative-tail sign is **venue-, window- and definition-dependent**, which is the standing instrument-dependence prior applied to the event definition rather than to the instrument. **Fourteenth-cycle update: the question is now also a retrieval question, and the answer to it is a determinate negative.** A reinstatement attempt would be strongest if it could show the *mechanism* directly — that an extreme funding state is followed by forced unwinds, on the entry's own instrument, in the same window as the claim — rather than inferring it. The record needed for that is free and dated on Binance's inverse family (2023-06-25 to 2024-10-14) and **was** free and dated on the entry's specified USDⓈ-M instrument (2023-06-25 to 2024-03-31) before the publisher withdrew it; the archive no longer serves the USDⓈ-M files, and the publisher's own dated statements say the product is no longer provided. So the question that now outranks the fourteenth-cycle candidates is: **is there any copy of the withdrawn USDⓈ-M per-contract forced-liquidation files outside the publisher's bucket**, and if there is, does it extend past 2024-03-31? If not, the inverse-family record is the only per-event mechanism witness available, and a reinstatement attempt must then either (i) use it as a documented instrument substitution, with the transport assumption pre-committed and its falsification criterion stated (the natural candidate is the ratio of the two contracts' open interest, which the archive does not publish for a given date, and which may therefore be untestable and hence inadmissible), or (ii) restrict itself to the overlapping window 2023-06-25 to 2024-10-14, which is a *short* window relative to the 2019-12 to 2026-07 sample the failed test used. Either way the answer is not a reason to reopen the entry; it is the specification a reopening attempt would have to satisfy.

## CRYPTO-EDGE-003 — Cross-Venue Funding Dispersion (Fragmentation Relative Value), v1.8

1. Short name and version: Cross-Venue Funding Dispersion v1.3 (v1.1 demoted the sign from an assumption to an estimable parameter; v1.2 records the external measurement of the signal's
      half-life, the weakening of the price-discovery objection, and a construction change from an instantaneous threshold to a slow clock; v1.3 fixes the tenth cycle's cycle-10 validation
      specification, whose mandatory incremental bar is now prior art in the same family rather than a placebo, and records the tenth cycle's downgrade of evidence quality from 2/5 to
      1/5 on three independent preregistered findings that bear on the entry's construction1/5 on three independent preregistered findings that bear on the entry's construction; v1.4 is the eleventh cycle's amendment: it adds the mandatory delisting-settlement exclusion and forced-flat rule, which follows from the newly confirmed free venue announcement feed and from the venues' own disclosed settlement mechanics, and it records the eleventh cycle's access findings and the disagreement about the placement of the tenth cycle's evidence-quality downgrade. No dimension moves and the total stays at 52) rather than on its sign). **v1.5 is the thirteenth cycle's amendment**: it records that the paired comparison against unthresholded patient carry — the question the twelfth cycle listed as open — **has now been answered by four independent 2026 sources, and answered against the thresholded selection construction**; it records the harvest-versus-select result as a cost-side finding in the entry's own family, venue set and window; and it closes the twelfth cycle's item 17 sub-question. **No dimension moves and the total stays at 52.** **v1.6 is the fourteenth cycle's amendment**: it records a **determinate answer to the second-venue access question the twelfth and thirteenth cycles carried as open** — a second venue's **free, dated, keyless** history for both the carry leg and the price leg **does exist** (Hyperliquid's public info API: `fundingHistory` from 2023-05-11 and daily `candleSnapshot` from 2023-05-12, both verified live, HTTP 200, no key, no registration) — and it records, in the same breath, the **determinate negative on the leg the entry actually specifies**: the **centralised-venue** second leg is **not** obtainable free on a multi-year window, because Bybit's public archive has no funding and no kline directory (HTTP 404 on both), its premium-index and spot-index archives cover only 2019-10 to 2020-03 for eleven coins, and its funding REST returns HTTP 403 from this environment; and because OKX's public `funding-rate-history` returns only the most recent **100 settlements** (2026-09-17 to 2026-10-19, about 33 days) and **ignores** both its `before` and `after` pagination parameters, so the thirteenth cycle's recorded claim that "the cross-venue leg is constructible from Binance's dated funding-rate archive on one side and OKX's public REST on the other" is **superseded in place**. It adds a **mandatory venue-pair pre-registration** and a **mandatory declaration of the collateral and venue-class difference** to the validation plan, because the free second venue is a **USDC-margined on-chain decentralised exchange** and the entry's specified universe is **USDT-margined centralised perpetuals**, which is a different collateral and a different venue class, and the entry's own item 11 already records collateral-mix and depeg confounds. It records a **recorded disagreement** on free-data accessibility (4/5 → 5/5, total 55) with the reason the cycle declines it. **No dimension moves and the total stays at 52.**
2. One-sentence thesis: Extreme dispersion of perpetual funding rates across trading venues for the same asset identifies venue-specific positioning or liquidity imbalances that the integrated multi-venue market has not priced, so the venue with the anomalously high funding rate subsequently underperforms the cross-venue median perpetual on a 5-20 day horizon, with the funding differential persisting long enough to be partly harvestable.
3. Status: developing (shortlisted, rank 2). Revised in the seventh research cycle from 58 to 55; confidence held at low; unchanged in the ninth research cycle, which found no new
      direct evidence in either direction. **Downgraded in the tenth research cycle from 55 to 52 on evidence quality alone (2/5 to 1/5), with confidence held at low.** The registered
      prediction has not been tested and failed, so the entry is neither rejected nor re-specified away; what changed in the tenth cycle is the *standing of the entry's construction*.
      Three independent preregistered results in the same funding family now document that a selection rule (take the extreme dispersion, trade only then) is monotonically dominated by a
      harvest rule (hold every stream that pays), that the harvest rule's own income is approximately zero in the 2025-2026 regime on a point-in-time universe as well as a hindsight one,
      and that the entry's own predictive mapping has never been tested at all. The entry therefore has no supporting evidence and three independent adverse findings, which is what the
      evidence-quality dimension measures. See items 5, 6, 13, 14, 15, 16 and 17.
4. Proposed edge family and target market/universe: Cross-venue market-fragmentation / relative-value derivatives (segmented arbitrage, venue-specific liquidity and counterparty premia); BTC and ETH USDT-margined perpetuals on Binance, Bybit and OKX (extendable to other venues where funding history and volume filters are available).
5. Mechanism: Perpetual funding rates are strongly but not perfectly correlated across venues because arbitrage across venues is segmented: exchanges do not offer cross-margining, so an arbitrageur exploiting a funding differential must fund and margin both legs separately, tying up capital on each venue and limiting the capital that can respond to any given discrepancy; venues also differ substantially in liquidity, and a venue-specific shock (withdrawal of market makers, collateral or stablecoin stress, venue-specific news) can produce a local imbalance. The literature documents that this residual dispersion persists: He, Manela, Ross & von Wachter (2026) report Binance-Bybit funding correlation of 0.74 with meaningful divergences during unusually large funding payments, and state explicitly that the residual cross-exchange dispersion reflects the limits of arbitrage across segmented venues and is a promising avenue for future research; Zhivkov (2026) finds that transaction costs and spread-reversal risks prevent arbitrage from eliminating large mispricings between platforms and that market frictions show up in cross-platform spreads rather than average levels, with persistent two-tier structure in which centralized venues lead price discovery. Two distinct stories can generate a high funding rate at one venue - locally crowded longs that will be force-unwound, or a locally scarce instrument whose market makers have withdrawn - and both imply the expensive venue's perpetual is biased rich relative to the integrated price; a third, contrary story is that the high-funding venue is the price-leading, information-rich venue, in which case the expensive venue should outperform. The hypothesis asserts the first two and is falsified by the third. **Amendment (v1.1): the third story is now the better-supported one, not the minority one.** Three independent sources place the price-discovery role on the more heavily traded, typically higher-funding venue rather than the thin one: Gornall, Rinaldi and Xiao (2025) report that perpetuals lead spot price discovery; Zhivkov (2026) finds that centralized venues lead price discovery with 61 percent higher information integration than decentralized venues and that information flow runs CEX to DEX with no reverse causality; and He, Manela, Ross and von Wachter (2026) attribute the residual cross-exchange funding dispersion to segmented arbitrage and liquidity differences. The mechanism that would make the *expensive* venue underperform therefore requires the thin venue to be the one with locally crowded longs, while the mechanism that makes the expensive venue *outperform* requires only that the expensive venue is the price leader — which is what the published evidence describes. A third, newly documented possibility (v1.1): the dispersion may be a *liquidity-withdrawal artifact* rather than a positioning imbalance at all, and the closest adjacent preregistered test of cross-venue disagreement as a magnitude found no predictive content whatsoever (see item 15). **Amendment (v1.2): the object the signal thresholds is documented to be fast-moving, and the price-discovery objection is documented to be weaker and less simple than v1.1 recorded.** Two independent external sources measure the half-life of a cross-venue funding differential directly, on free public data. A 2026 cross-venue study covering July 2024 to July 2026 across Binance, Bybit, OKX and Hyperliquid finds the exchange-versus-DEX funding spread mean-reverting with an AR(1) half-life of 1.5 to 3.5 hours, while the *direction* of the spread - which venue pays more - persists for months, and concludes explicitly that the signal's direction is a regime that lasts months while its level is hour-to-hour noise. The 2026 microstructure study by Zhivkov, Todorov and Georgiev, on 9.1 million hourly observations across 26 exchanges and 812 symbols from November 2025 to January 2026, finds intraday funding-spread autocorrelations above 0.96 with ETH reverting in about 20 minutes and other symbols taking hours to days, and finds that spreads peak roughly two hours after each eight-hour settlement and decay afterwards, with arbitrage prevalence of 21.4% at the 02:00 UTC peak against 16.5% at the 17:00 UTC trough. Two consequences follow, and they point in opposite directions. (a) Against the entry: the registered signal thresholds the *level* of the dispersion against a trailing distribution, and a quantity with a one-to-three-hour half-life means a trailing-decile extreme is close to trading noise, so the construction may be selecting on the wrong component of the phenomenon. (b) In favour of the entry: the price-discovery objection that has been the entry's primary opposing argument - 'the expensive venue is the price leader, so it outperforms' - is not a size story. The same 2026 study finds Bybit Granger-causing Binance funding rates in 62% of rolling windows against 21% in the reverse direction, and OKX Granger-causing Binance in 55% against 25%, and concludes that information discovery does not follow a size hierarchy and that mid-tier centralised venues and even decentralised venues contain information that propagates to larger platforms. A venue can therefore be expensive *and* informative for reasons unrelated to being the largest, and the entry's 'thin venue with locally crowded longs' story is not excluded by the objection. The v1.1 statement that the third story is 'now the better-supported one' is therefore withdrawn as a general claim: the evidence supports price leadership in the CEX-versus-DEX tier, and mid-tier leadership over the largest venue, and supports neither of the specific readings the entry's sign question needs.
6. Exact falsifiable prediction: Define d_v,t = (venue-v 8-hour funding rate for symbol s at funding timestamp t, normalized to a common 8-hour-equivalent basis) minus (the cross-venue median funding rate for the same symbol at the same timestamp). When d_v,t is in the top decile of its trailing 90-day distribution (and, in the mirrored case, the bottom decile), the venue-v perpetual's return over the next 5 to 20 days is below (above) the median return of the same symbol's perpetuals across the other venues, and the funding differential itself does not fully close within one to three funding intervals. Direction: high relative funding -> relative underperformance of the expensive venue. Horizon: 5-20 days for relative performance; 1-3 funding intervals for dispersion persistence. **Amendment (v1.1): the sign is now a parameter to be estimated, not an assumption to be tested.** The registered direction above is the contrarian reading. The weight of evidence has shifted toward the continuation reading (the expensive venue is the price leader and outperforms), so a validator must estimate the sign before trading it: the mandatory first step is a two-sided estimation of E[relative return of the expensive venue | extreme dispersion] with a block-bootstrap interval, and the trade direction must follow the estimated sign, not the registered one. If the estimated interval excludes zero on the continuation side, the contrarian trade in this entry is closed and the entry should be rewritten as a continuation hypothesis or rejected. The dispersion-persistence sub-claim (1-3 funding intervals) is unaffected by the sign question and should be tested independently, because it is the part of the hypothesis that the published microstructure evidence actually addresses. **Amendment (v1.2): the sign remains an estimable parameter; the persistence sub-claim is now contested; the construction is re-registered on a slow clock.** (a) The sign is still to be estimated two-sided and sign-free, with a block-bootstrap interval, before any trade expression is specified, exactly as in v1.1. Nothing located in this cycle establishes the sign in either direction, and the price-discovery objection that v1.1 treated as the primary opposing argument is now less settled than v1.1 recorded. (b) The dispersion-persistence sub-claim - that the funding differential does not fully close within one to three funding intervals - now has direct external evidence pointing the other way for the *level* of the differential: the two-year, four-venue study finds a half-life of 1.5 to 3.5 hours for the exchange-versus-DEX spread, and the microstructure study finds intraday spread autocorrelations above 0.96 with ETH reverting in about 20 minutes. The two published results differ in venue pair (CEX-versus-DEX versus CEX-versus-CEX), symbol and window, so the honest reading is that persistence is an *open* empirical question whose answer is venue-pair dependent, not that it is false. It must still be tested and reported separately from the sign question, because it does not depend on the sign. (c) Because the documented structure is that the *direction* of the cross-venue differential is a slow regime while its *level* is fast noise, a validator must register the signal on a slow clock - a trailing-window measure of which venue has been persistently expensive, rebalanced on a weekly-to-monthly cadence - rather than on an instantaneous or trailing-decile level threshold. This is a change of construction, not of claim. It is permitted here under the falsification-handling rule because the entry's registered prediction has not been tested and failed; the earlier instantaneous-threshold specification is retained in this ledger as the version that was registered, and the slow-clock specification is recorded as a new specification with its own bars, so that neither can be quietly swapped for the other. (d) The falsification criteria are unchanged in substance - dispersion closes fully within one funding interval, or the expensive venue does not underperform on average, or the effect disappears after volume and open-interest filtering and mark-price substitution, or the effect vanishes after residualizing against momentum and basis, or the reversed-sign placebo performs as well as the stated signal - with a fifth added: the slow-clock specification earns nothing over a no-trade book on the same venues and dates, which is the specific failure mode the family precedent documented (a statistically significant information signal that died on execution).
7. Candidate explanatory variables and proposed signal concept: Signal = cross-venue funding dispersion d_v,t (a relative, venue-demeaned quantity), computed only from funding rates settled at or before timestamp t; venue pair selection and all thresholds from trailing distributions only; forward window starts after the funding settlement. Plain language: "is this one venue's perpetual unusually expensive relative to the same asset priced elsewhere?" Because the measure differences out the common component of carry - which He et al. show is largely momentum-driven and co-moving across currencies - it targets venue-specific segmentation rather than market-wide carry.
8. Expected trade expression: Market-neutral relative value: short the expensive venue's perpetual and long the cheap venue's perpetual (or hedge the short with spot on the same venue), equal notional, no directional beta; the funding received on the short leg offsets carry paid on the long leg when the differential is positive. Not a directional long/short market bet. Feasibility caveats: margin must be posted on both venues, collateral must exist on both, and venue or collateral risk (for example a stablecoin depeg or venue insolvency) breaks the hedge rather than being diversifiable.
9. Primary comparator or null explanation: (i) a random venue pair traded with the same notional and dates; (ii) the same trade restricted to low-dispersion dates; (iii) the liquidity-artifact null - the dispersion must survive filtering venues by volume and open interest and using mark prices rather than last trades; (iv) the momentum null - the signal must add value after residualizing against trailing returns and against CRYPTO-EDGE-001 basis; (v) the price-discovery null, in which the expensive venue leads and the trade should be reversed; (vi) a sign placebo using the mirrored dispersion signal to detect implementation errors.
10. Data required, source URLs, free-access classification, granularity, likely history, coverage, limitations: per-venue 8-hour funding history and per-venue perpetual mark prices and klines for the same symbol; per-venue volume/open interest for liquidity filters. Verified free public sources (checked 2026-10-20): Binance GET https://fapi.binance.com/fapi/v1/fundingRate (public, no key, limit max 1000, returns fundingRate, fundingTime, markPrice; funding interval/cap/floor adjustments via GET /fapi/v1/fundingInfo) - access 1 for endpoint availability, 2 for multi-year pagination depth; Bybit GET https://api.bybit.com/v5/market/funding/history (public, no key, 200 rows per page, covers USDT/USDC/inverse perps) - access 1; OKX GET https://www.okx.com/api/v5/public/funding-rate-history (public, capped at 100 rows per call) - access 1 for endpoint availability, 2 for retention depth; Binance public data archive https://data.binance.vision (documented at https://github.com/binance/binance-public-data) for monthly/daily klines and trades - access 1. Limitations: funding intervals, caps and floors differ across venues and must be normalized (Binance /fapi/v1/fundingInfo exists for this); collateral mixes (USDT vs USDC) and stablecoin depegs can masquerade as basis; venue survivorship (FTX, OKEx renames) must be handled; thin-venue quotes and stale mark prices create artificial dispersion; funding timestamps must be aligned across venues with different settlement cadences.
11. Plausible friction and capacity considerations (theoretical, no measured values): the trade needs margin on two venues, so capital efficiency is roughly halved versus a single-venue trade; taker fees on both legs and possible slippage in exactly the stressed conditions that trigger the signal; funding accrual is the main expected return but Zhivkov (2026) documents that among top cross-venue opportunities only about 40 percent remained profitable after transaction costs and spread reversals, with forced exits in about 95 percent of opportunities - so the pure carry-capture version of this trade is likely unprofitable at retail scale and the hypothesis is stated as a directional relative-performance claim instead; transfer and counterparty risk between venues is unhedgeable; capacity is limited by the depth of the smaller venue.
12. Why meaningfully different from the catalogue and prior art: CRYPTO-EDGE-001 is an absolute time-series carry level on a benchmark instrument aimed at crash timing; CRYPTO-EDGE-002 is a single-venue funding percentile contrarian rule; CRYPTO-EDGE-003 is a relative, cross-sectional, market-neutral measure of venue-specific fragmentation that differences out the market-wide carry and momentum component. Novelty is scoped to this catalogue and to the searches performed (see SOURCE_NOTES.md): the primary literature explicitly identifies residual cross-exchange funding dispersion as an open research avenue rather than a studied effect, and the directional relative-performance mapping from dispersion to venue-level underperformance has not been found in the free sources consulted. It is not claimed to be globally novel.
13. Strongest argument against; likely failure regimes; confounders: **Ninth-cycle adversarial duplicate check against the documented patient cross-venue carry construction (carried over from the eighth cycle's instruction and now performed) - and this is the sharpest objection to the entry as it stands.** The cost evidence forces the entry onto a slow clock, and the one construction that is documented to clear costs on a slow clock is *unthresholded*: continuously holding and revisiting only which venue to short on a 30-day clock returned +2.7% to +5.4% annualized net with a maximum drawdown under 2%, and the dispersion *level* itself was found to have a 1.5-3.5 hour half-life and to be hour-to-hour noise whose "direction is a regime that lasts months". So the construction the cost evidence pushes towards is the documented patient carry construction, and the only content that distinguishes this entry from it is the **extreme-dispersion threshold**, which is exactly the part documented to be a net loss (-9% to -15% annualized after costs) and to be trading noise. That is a genuine prior that this entry's remaining distinct content is small, and it is why the ninth cycle held the entry at 55 rather than raising it: the slow-clock construction the cost evidence selects is the construction the prior art already owns. A second, smaller point: the entry is therefore *not* a duplicate of the documented patient carry construction, because it retains a threshold and a sign claim - but it is a conditional overlay on top of a strategy whose net-of-cost value is already documented, so the overlay has to earn its keep against a benchmark of zero rather than against a negative one. (i) The signal may be a liquidity-supply artifact rather than a positioning imbalance - if market makers withdraw from a venue, funding there rises, and the premium may persist or widen rather than revert, making the trade a persistent cost drag. (ii) The sign may be the opposite of the stated prediction if the high-funding venue is the price-discovery leader (Gornall, Rinaldi & Xiao 2025 report perpetuals lead spot price discovery; Ackerer, Hugonnier & Jermann document perpetual pricing and price discovery effects), in which case buying the expensive venue and shorting the cheap one is the profitable expression. **Promoted to the primary objection in v1.1:** this is no longer the minority reading. It is corroborated by Zhivkov (2026) on CEX-to-DEX information flow with no reverse causality, by He et al. (2026) on liquidity-driven dispersion, and by a preregistered cross-venue test in which the high-premium venue was followed by *higher* returns in the continuation direction (+3.17% at 7 days, CI [+0.51%, +6.08%], breadth 17/20, on 31,752 symbol-days, surviving a trailing-return trim and a concentration check). If the sign is inverted, the trade in this entry loses money by construction and the entry is falsified in its registered form. (iii) Structural, permanent venue premia (fee tiers, funding caps, collateral composition, jurisdictional risk) may dominate the transient component and make cross-venue mean reversion a losing trade. (iv) Crowded-liquidity episodes can produce dispersion that resolves through a liquidation cascade at the expensive venue, where the relative move is large but the execution risk and slippage are worst - the classic adverse-selection problem. (v) Statistical power: extreme dispersion days are rare, so the effective sample is small and multiple-testing risk is high across venue pairs, symbols and thresholds. (vi) Data quality: mark-price staleness on thin venues, funding-interval mismatches, and stablecoin depegs can manufacture dispersion. (vii, new in v1.1) The closest adjacent empirical test of this entry's family found that cross-venue disagreement *as a magnitude* carries no forward-return content at all: in a preregistered test of three cross-venue price signals (raw premium, peg-adjusted premium, and the standard deviation of the three venues' log-USD prices), the dispersion signal was noise at all three horizons (1-day +0.08%, CI [-0.33%, +0.56%]; 7-day -0.24%, CI [-2.34%, +1.90%]; 30-day -6.65%, CI [-16.81%, +2.24%]). That test was on *price* dispersion at daily close rather than on *funding* dispersion, so it does not directly falsify this entry, but it removes dispersion magnitude as a self-evident predictor and means the funding-dispersion version now has to earn its own evidence. (viii, new in v1.1) Even a statistically significant information signal in this family may not be a tradable edge: the continuation signal from (ii) was carried into a strategy-grade test with next-open fills, fees, spread and participation impact, and was killed — Sharpe 0.89 against 1.0 required, deflated Sharpe 0.36 against 0.95 required, and it lost to an equal-weight buy-and-hold of the same basket on the same window and engine (Sharpe 0.94). That failure was for a long-only beta-heavy expression rather than for a delta-neutral relative trade, so it is an analogy and not a direct refutation, but it is a documented precedent for this exact family. Likely failure regimes: venue-specific distress, stablecoin depeg, regime changes in leverage caps, and periods when arbitrage capital is unconstrained (tight dispersion). (ix, new in v1.2) The signal may be measuring the wrong component of the phenomenon. The dispersion *level* has a documented one-to-three-hour half-life in a two-year, four-venue sample and the dispersion *direction* is a slow regime; a trailing-decile threshold on the level therefore selects on fast noise, and the entry's registered construction may fail for a reason that has nothing to do with its mechanism - it may simply have no power, in which case the test is not falsifying the mechanism but the construction, and a null result would be uninformative. (x, new in v1.2) The sharpest external test of the *family's* construction was not the sign test but a cost test, and it is directly adverse to this entry's registered construction: a two-year study of cross-venue funding carry across three centralised venues and one decentralised venue finds that a threshold rule which enters when the trailing spread clears a preregistered level earns the largest *gross* income of any rule tested and finishes at -9% to -15% annualized after costs, with execution consuming roughly three times the gross, while the rule that barely trades - hold continuously and revisit only which venue to short on a 30-day clock - returns +2.7% to +5.4% annualized net on notional with a maximum drawdown under 2%. Those are the entry's two candidate constructions and the documented outcome of the entry's own construction is a loss after costs. (xi, new in v1.2) Cross-asset heterogeneity of funding-dispersion effects is now documented and cuts both ways: a six-asset, three-venue study of funding extremes found the top-decile state predicting a negative median 72-hour return on BTC but positive medians on ETH, BNB, XRP and DOGE, so a claim stated over a multi-asset universe inherits a sign that depends on the asset mix; and a conditional study of 274 funding-extreme events found the response flipping with the direction of the market on the event day (reversal on up days, continuation on down days), which is a warning that a pooled estimate averages over two different regimes. (xii, new in v1.2) The strongest *supportive* finding for the entry is also new: the price-discovery objection, which v1.1 promoted to primary, is now documented to be a poor general rule, because mid-tier centralised venues and decentralised venues lead the largest venue in funding-rate information flow. The entry's contrarian story is therefore not excluded by the objection, but it remains entirely unsupported in its own right.
**Thirteenth-cycle addition, and it is the strongest cost-side objection the entry has yet faced: the thresholded cross-venue selection has now been documented to lose money after costs in two independent tests, while the unthresholded patient harvest has been documented to make money in the same family.** (i) A public GitHub study of the exact trade expression the entry specifies — long the venue with the more negative trailing funding, short the venue with the more positive, delta-neutral, per asset, re-decided on the settlement clock — over 18,208 hourly observations per pair for BTC, ETH and SOL across Binance, Bybit, OKX and Hyperliquid from July 2024 to July 2026, finds that the **threshold rule** (enter when the trailing spread clears 10% annualised) **earns the most gross income of any rule tested and finishes at -9% to -15% annualised after costs**, that the spread's level mean-reverts with a half-life of 1.5-3.5 hours against the 100-135 hours a 13 basis-point round trip needs to repay itself, and that the rule which barely trades — holding continuously and revisiting only *which* venue to short, on a 30-day clock — returns **+2.7% to +5.4% annualised net with a maximum drawdown under 2%**. (ii) A preregistered 11-gate public test of the same archetype in June 2026 **retired** it, at 3 of 11 gates and a profit factor of 0.107, with the author stating that "the archetype dies on COST, not on no-edge" and that the gross signal was shredded by roughly 86% per year of flip cost; that test's own venue-availability note records the same free-data constraint this catalogue records — Binance's global API geoblocked, no Coinbase perpetual funding — leaving only Hyperliquid and dYdX, which is why its two-venue configuration "reduces 'most negative versus most positive' to long-cheaper/short-dearer of two legs". (iii) A third source, a working paper with an open replication package on Zenodo, finds a cross-venue dislocation worth roughly 7% annualised that is walk-forward consistent and survives a Deflated Sharpe with multiple-testing correction, and reports that **every "smart" refinement — cost-recovery exits, cross-sectional allocation, machine-learning return tilts and tail-risk leverage — fails to beat a naive baseline**, several of them significantly. (iv) The peer-reviewed source already in the catalogue, read in full this cycle, reports that of the economically significant arbitrage spreads it identifies only 40% generate positive returns after transaction costs and spread reversals, and that forced exits occur in 95% of opportunities.
**What this does and does not do to the entry.** It does *not* test the entry's registered prediction, which is about the *price* differential (does the venue with the anomalously high funding rate underperform the cross-venue median perpetual over 5-20 days), and the falsification-handling rule is therefore **not** engaged: the entry is not downgraded for a failed test, because no test of its registered claim has been run. What it does is corroborate, from four independent dated sources including one peer-reviewed, that the entry's *cost case* is exactly as weak as its floor score of 1/5 said, and that the prior in this family runs in one direction only: the harvest arm carries the documented positive net result and the select arm carries the documented net loss. The entry's marginal value is therefore now clearly located in one place — the **sign** of the venue-relative return, which remains untested — and nowhere else.
14. Research-only validation plan to be executed elsewhere: preregister the venue universe (with liquidity filters fixed in advance), the extreme-dispersion threshold (trailing 90-day decile of d_v,t), the holding window (5-20 days) and the persistence window (1-3 funding intervals); enter at the funding settlement after observation, sized delta-neutral across the two venues; comparators as in item 9, including the reversed-sign placebo; cost model must include taker fees on both legs, funding accrual on both legs, slippage, and the capital cost of posting margin on two venues; out-of-sample design with time-based splits and a leave-one-venue-out test (fit on some venues, trade the held-out venue); regime coverage across trending bull, bear, high-volatility and liquidation-event windows; falsification criteria - dispersion closes fully within one funding interval, or the expensive venue does not underperform on average, or the effect disappears after volume/OI filtering and mark-price substitution, or the effect vanishes after residualizing against momentum and basis, or the reversed-sign placebo performs as well as the stated signal. **Amended in v1.1:** (a) the plan is reordered so that the *sign* is estimated before any trade expression is specified — a two-sided block-bootstrap estimate of the expensive-versus-cheap venue relative return conditional on extreme dispersion, reported for both signs, with the trade direction chosen by the estimate rather than by the registration; (b) the mean-difference statistic must be reported alongside the *volatility of the bucket*, because a documented precedent in this family showed that a significant conditional-mean spread can coexist with a strategy Sharpe below the bar when the bucket is thin (two signals with identical spreads are not equally valuable if one is carried by fifteen names and the other by 1.4); (c) the dispersion-persistence sub-claim is tested as a separate, sign-free hypothesis; (d) inference must be heavy-tail aware (Grobys and Shahzad 2026: crypto factor realised variances follow power laws and the population mean and variance of such a factor are statistically not defined under block bootstrap), so the validator should report median-based and block-bootstrap summaries and treat a Sharpe ratio as uninformative for the underlying factor.
