The entry's registered mapping is untouched by this — it is about the *price* differential, not the funding capture — so the entry is not rejected and not re-specified; but the question has lost its force as a discriminator, because the construction-level cost problem is now settled independently of the sign.
**The question that replaces it is narrower and sharper.** (i) *Does the venue-relative price differential exist at all on a slow clock?* The entry's registered prediction is that the venue with the anomalously high funding rate underperforms the cross-venue median perpetual over 5-20 days. Every source found in the thirteenth cycle tests either a *static* cross-venue premium (Lau: 7% annualised, real and harvestable) or a *carry capture* (AyusoPita, Nguyen, the preregistered ledger). None tests whether the expensive venue's *price* converges downward against the median, and the one thing the sources do establish is that a *harvest* of the same differential is profitable without any price convergence at all. So the entry's mapping may be redundant with its own funding capture: if the differential is harvestable as carry, the price-convergence claim is not needed to make money, and an entry whose *registered* payoff is redundant with a documented alternative is worth less than its score implies. The decisive test is therefore a **two-sided, sign-free estimation of the venue-relative price return on a slow clock, reported alongside the carry-only alternative on the same clock, window, cost model and inference** — and the null must be stated in advance: if the price differential adds nothing to the carry-only alternative, the entry's registered mapping is not needed and the entry should be **closed on redundancy** rather than on a wrong-signed return test. (ii) *Is there a second venue whose free dated history covers the same window?* This is now the only material free-data obstacle, it is the same obstacle the preregistered 11-gate test recorded, and the cycle notes that Binance's own dated funding archive removes the Binance leg's blocker while leaving the second venue's history to OKX's REST and Hyperliquid's on-chain record. (iii) *Does the delisting-settlement exclusion and forced-flat rule, added in v1.4, change the cost arithmetic?* With the harvest arm now documented as the profitable one, the forced-flat rule's opportunity cost — giving up the carry on the delisting venue at the moment the funding is best — becomes a *first-order* term rather than a housekeeping detail, and it should be priced into the comparison rather than applied as an unpriced constraint.

**Fourteenth-cycle update: the second-venue leg of this entry's open question is now settled in both directions, and the settlement narrows rather than widens the entry's specification.** The twelfth and thirteenth cycles carried "the cross-venue half is still REST-only" as an open obstacle, with OKX's REST the presumed route. The cycle's determinations are that (i) **a free, dated, keyless second venue now exists** — Hyperliquid's public info API, funding from 2023-05-11 and daily price from 2023-05-12, both verified live — so the "second venue's history" blocker is removed *for a decentralised, USDC-margined venue*; and (ii) **the centralised second leg is not free and was never going to be** — OKX's public funding endpoint returns about 33 days and ignores `before` and `after`, and Bybit's public archive publishes no funding and no klines — so the route the thirteenth cycle recorded is **superseded in place** and the "Binance leg's blocker removed while the second venue's history is left to OKX's REST" sentence in the thirteenth cycle's own update is now false in its second half. The two determinations together produce a sharper, smaller question than the one they replace. (i) *Is a CEX-versus-DEX pair a valid instantiation of the entry's registered prediction, or does the registered claim require two centralised venues?* The entry's thesis is about the **integrated multi-venue market** and the failure of arbitrage across venue boundaries; a decentralised venue with a different collateral, a different participant base and a documented 1.5-3.5 hour spread half-life is a *stronger* case of fragmentation than two centralised venues, which is a point in favour of the substitution, but it is also a different claim, and the entry's own persistence sub-claim would then be tested on the pair where it is most likely to fail. The decision is a **specification** decision that should be made in advance and recorded, not chosen after seeing which pair produces a result. (ii) *What does the entry do about the liquidity filter on the second venue, given that free dated open interest is not available there?* The item 9(iii) control uses venue volume and open interest; on the decentralised leg, volume is free and dated through the daily candles and open interest is not, so the filter must be either volume-only, or asymmetric with the asymmetry declared, or applied to the centralised leg alone — and each variant is a different test. (iii) *Does the entry's *paired comparison* against unthresholded patient carry survive the venue-pair change?* The harvest arm is the documented profitable construction and the entry's content is the incremental sign of the selection rule over it; the fourteenth cycle's answer is that this pairing question is now answerable on free data for the first time, because both venues' funding and price histories are free and dated over 2023-05 to 2026-10, and the *first* thing a validator should do with that is not the sign test but the paired comparison, since the entry closes unless the thresholded rule beats the harvest rule on the same slow clock, in the same window, with the same costs and the same inference.

## CRYPTO-EDGE-004 — Dispersion-Contingent Momentum Breakdown (Cross-Sectional Disagreement as a Momentum-Reliability Switch), v1.8 — REINSTATED (active, rank 3)

1. Short name and version: Dispersion-Contingent Momentum Breakdown v1.3 (v1.0 was the entry as added in the sixth research cycle; v1.1 recorded the free-data determination for the
      essential input, the prior-art position of the claim, and moved the entry to deferred status; v1.2 lifts the deferral because the data determination changed, records the free
      construction that removes the blocker, and re-scores free-data accessibility up and net value after frictions down; v1.3 corrects the *description* of that construction after an
      adversarial re-verification in the tenth cycle, completes the cycle-10 validation specification, and records two new peer-reviewed sources that sharpen the entry's competing
      explanation without moving any dimension score). **Eleventh-cycle additions to the data determination - and a correction to a recorded access fact that is material to this entry.** (a) **The recorded listing-pagination rule for the Binance archive is wrong, and following it produces false terminal dates.** The catalogue's access note states that the S3 listing endpoint "truncates at 1000 keys per response and, when no delimiter is used, paginates with `<NextContinuationMarker>` rather than `<NextMarker>`". Direct inspection of the live response on 2026-10-20 shows this is **not** what the endpoint does. With **no** delimiter the response contains **no continuation element of any kind** - the only marker element present is `<Marker>`, which merely echoes the parameter supplied - and it is truncated with `<IsTruncated>true</IsTruncated>`. With **a** delimiter the response does carry a standard `<NextMarker>`. The working method for listing files is therefore: **no delimiter, and page by supplying `marker=<the last key returned>`**; verified end-to-end on 2026-10-20, where a second request with `marker=IPUSDT-1d-2026-06-27.zip.CHECKSUM` returned the next 228 keys ending at IPUSDT-1d-2026-10-19. A validator who follows the recorded rule finds no `<NextContinuationMarker>`, concludes the listing is complete, and silently takes the **first** 1000 keys. The consequence is not a truncated sample but a **wrong answer**: the first page ends at the symbol's 500th daily file, so the naive "last file" is a date in the middle of the symbol's life. Measured on 20 sampled symbols, **20 of 20 were truncated**, and the naive terminal dates are mid-life dates - BTCUSDT 2021-05-13, ETHUSDT 2021-05-13, SOLUSDT 2022-01-26, DOGEUSDT 2021-11-21, XRPUSDT 2021-05-23, ADAUSDT 2021-06-13, AVAXUSDT 2022-02-04, LINKUSDT 2021-05-30, TONUSDT 2025-07-13, TRXUSDT 2021-05-28, NEARUSDT 2022-02-26, APTUSDT 2024-03-01, ARBUSDT 2024-08-03, LTCUSDT 2021-05-22, BCHUSDT 2021-05-13, ETCUSDT 2021-05-29, FILUSDT 2022-02-27, ATOMUSDT 2021-06-20, INJUSDT 2023-12-29, SUIUSDT 2024-09-13. For this entry the failure mode is severe and specific: a universe built on the recorded rule would treat **still-trading contracts as delisted** at a mid-sample date, which is a survivorship error in the *opposite* direction to the one the entry was reinstated to fix, and it would corrupt both the point-in-time membership and the momentum ranking in the same place. (b) **The terminal-date rule is now validated against an independent reference, and the tenth cycle's description of the terminal states is refuted.** The venue's own dated delisting notices are an independent reference for the last trading date. Six contract instances across four notices were read directly from the 1d kline zips on 2026-10-20: IPUSDT and IPUSDC (announced 2026-06-25, settlement 2026-06-28 09:00 UTC), PROMPTUSDT and 1000000BOBUSDT (announced 2026-10-01, settlement 2026-10-05 09:00 UTC), and RVVUSDT and YALAUSDT (announced 2026-02-10, settlement 2026-02-10 09:00 UTC). In **every** case the archive's last non-zero-trade date equals the announced settlement date exactly, and in every case the file series **continues after settlement** with zero volume, zero trades and a frozen close (IPUSDT: 61,146 trades on 2026-06-28, then zero on 2026-06-29, 2026-07-01 and 2026-10-19 with the close frozen; PROMPTUSDT: 78,760 trades on 2026-10-05, then zero from 2026-10-06; 1000000BOBUSDT: 1,201,964 trades on 2026-10-05, then zero from 2026-10-06; RVVUSDT: 290,530 trades on 2026-02-10, then zero from 2026-02-11; YALAUSDT: 269,688 trades on 2026-02-10, then zero from 2026-02-11). The tenth cycle's taxonomy - a delisted contract's series ends, while an inactive-but-not-delisted contract's series continues - is therefore **refuted**: both of the contracts checked here were *delisted* and both continue, while the tenth cycle's LUNAUSDT case was delisted and ended. The rule (last trading date = the last date with a non-zero trade count) survives, is now independently validated, and the correct description is that the terminal *shape* is a property of the venue's file-emission behaviour and is unrelated to delisting status. (c) **A second, independent source of delisting dates now exists, which removes the entry's dependence on a single construction.** The Binance delisting-announcement feed enumerates 442 delisting articles back to 2022-02-24, of which 76 are perpetual-contract notices, each with the exact settlement datetime and the exact contract symbols. The entry's universe construction can therefore be built from the *announcement* dates and *checked* against the archive's trade counts, rather than being derived from the archive alone, and the two must agree. The two-sided disagreement is a construction check, not a tie-break. (d) **The archive's update log is not exhaustive, and its coverage of the futures tree is not established.** The README's "Updates" table contains exactly two rows (2022-08-08 klines, 2022-04-21 aggregate trades) and claims to be "an exhaustive list of updates performed to the archive", but the repository's `updates/` directory contains a **third** artefact, `2022-10-04_aggregate_trade_updates.csv`, which the table does not list; and the enumeration of replaced files lives in those artefacts in the **GitHub repository**, not in the S3 bucket, whose `updates/` prefix is empty. The `.CHECKSUM` mechanism does work - the checksum file for BTCUSDT-1d-2026-10-19.zip contains the file's SHA-256 and was verified byte-for-byte against the downloaded zip on 2026-10-20 - and the 2022-08-08 kline event covers **1,659 spot symbols**, so the "versioned data" rule must be narrowed to the **spot** tree on the evidence available; the versioning status of the **futures** tree, which is the tree this entry and the other two active entries use, is **not established**. The tenth cycle's characterisation of the archive as publishing an exhaustive dated update log is therefore **superseded in place** by this narrower, verified statement. explanation without moving any dimension score). **v1.5 is the thirteenth cycle's amendment**: it records that the entry's own **base effect** is now documented in a **peer-reviewed** source (Han, Kang and Ryu, *Review of Asset Pricing Studies*, published 13 October 2026) as close to non-existent, which is adverse and does not move a score; it records that a **public executable replication of the entry's own construction** exists and that it depends on a **paid data tier**, which sharpens the existing free-data determination; and it records a new size-composition confounder. **No dimension moves and the total stays at 45.** **v1.6 is the fourteenth cycle's amendment**: it **verifies the size-momentum source** the thirteenth cycle carried as an unverified lead, which is now located and dated — **Xu, Zezhou and Wu, Fenglin, "Size-Momentum Puzzle in Cryptocurrencies", SSRN 6628860, published 1 January 2026** (a working-paper preprint, so the catalogue's evidence standing for it is unchanged) — and it records **two independent corroborations of the same size-dependence of the crypto momentum sign** (Liu, Tsyvinski and Wu, NBER working paper w25882, and Kozlowski, Puleo and Zhou, "Cryptocurrency return reversals"), which together turn the thirteenth cycle's *hypothetical* size-composition confounder into a **documented, triply corroborated** one; and it adds the **mandatory size-composition declaration and size-matched control** that the confounder now requires, without which a free replication's null is uninterpretable because the free screen and the published screen can select populations with **opposite signs of the base effect**. **No dimension moves and the total stays at 45**, for the reason recorded in item 16.
2. One-sentence thesis: When the cross-section of crypto assets is unusually dispersed in its returns — that is, when coins disagree most sharply about direction — the cross-sectional ranking of trailing returns becomes dominated by asset-specific noise rather than by continuation, so the long-winners/short-losers momentum spread collapses and momentum exposure should be cut or reversed in that state.
3. Status: **developing (shortlisted, rank 3)** — shortlisted at rank 3 in the sixth research cycle, moved off the active shortlist in the seventh, and **reinstated in the ninth** when
      the data ground for the deferral was removed. Total 45/100, confidence low. The entry is still not rejected: its registered prediction has not been tested and failed, so the
      falsification-handling rule is not engaged. What changed is that the essential input is now constructible on free data - and, in the opposite direction, that the base effect the entry
      switches on has been measured net of costs on a tradable universe and found not to survive. Reinstatement conditions are recorded in item 17 and in REJECTED_AND_DEFERRED.md.
      **Tenth cycle: unchanged at 45/100, confidence low.** The cycle's contribution to this entry was adversarial re-verification of the construction (which corrected the
      construction's *description* while leaving its *rule* intact) and the completion of a preregisterable cycle-10 specification, plus two new peer-reviewed sources that bear on the
      entry's competing explanation. No dimension moves: no new source tests the entry's registered prediction, and two of the entry's dimensions (robustness 1/5, net value after frictions
      1/5) are already at their floors.
4. Proposed edge family and target market/universe: Cross-sectional breadth / market-structure regime variable applied to cross-sectional momentum; a survivorship-aware universe of liquid USDT-quoted crypto spot pairs (BTC, ETH and the top ~50-100 tokens by contemporaneous market capitalisation and quote volume) on major venues (Binance, OKX, Bybit, Coinbase).
5. Mechanism: cross-sectional dispersion measures disagreement about the direction of the common factor, and it acts on the *reliability* of a ranking signal through two channels. (a) *Signal-to-noise channel*: the momentum payoff is delivered largely through the common (market) trend component; when dispersion is low, tokens co-move, the cross-sectional ranking is driven by differences in exposure and beta, and the ranking is stable and informative; when dispersion is high, the cross-section is dominated by idiosyncratic, asset-specific news (token unlocks, listings, meme rotations, idiosyncratic liquidations, protocol events), which raises the variance of the ranking error faster than it raises expected continuation, so the momentum spread's signal-to-noise ratio falls — and because crypto factor payoffs have power-law tails, a fall in signal-to-noise can push the mean below estimability rather than merely shrink it. (b) *Crowding channel*: high-dispersion states are also the states in which momentum positions are most crowded and most exposed to a common-factor reversal, so the momentum spread's *left tail* is fattest exactly in the high-dispersion regime. Why it may persist: the state variable is slow-moving relative to the formation window and is not itself the traded signal, so it does not attract the arbitrage that would erode the base momentum effect; it is a risk-budgeting switch on a known effect rather than an alpha source in its own right. Competing explanation that must be tested rather than assumed: dispersion may be a proxy for a *market-wide volatility regime* that independently damages momentum; the published evidence claims the effect is not explained by generic market volatility or by average cross-asset correlation, but a free replication must redo that orthogonalisation. **Amendment (v1.1): the state variable and the signal are unchanged; what changed is the universe in which the claim can be measured, and the answer to the entry's own open question about that universe is now known.** The v1.0 mechanism argued that cross-sectional dispersion raises the variance of the ranking error faster than it raises expected continuation, so that the momentum spread's signal-to-noise ratio falls. Two new findings bear on it. First, the measurement of the *base* effect: a preregistered audit of an unchanged momentum-rotation specification, re-run on a universe selected point-in-time rather than at the end of the sample, retained 58% of its Sharpe (1.47 to 0.86) and 49% of its CAGR (+42.91% to +21.06%), cleared neither a Sharpe of 1.0 nor a deflated-Sharpe bar of 0.95, and reversed its best year (2026: +92.80% to -18.61%). The momentum book that this entry proposes to switch is therefore materially weaker on an honest universe, and the degradation concentrated in exactly the years when a coin that later became famous was mid-parabola - that is, in the parabolic, high-dispersion years. Second, the *conditional* claim: a preregistered test in the same ledger conditioned the cross-sectional momentum spread on the cross-sectional dispersion of trailing 30-day returns - the same state variable, the same book, a different universe, terciles instead of deciles and a 7-day forward window instead of a 1-month one - and it was killed, with the spread monotone *increasing* across dispersion terciles (+0.50%, +1.05%, +1.40%) and a top-minus-bottom difference of +0.90% with a 95% block-bootstrap interval of [-2.66%, +3.69%]. The observed direction is the opposite of this entry's registered prediction, which says the spread should be *smaller* in the high-dispersion regime. The test is not decisive - the interval is wide, the universe was a hindsight one, and the state variable was measured daily rather than on a trailing window - but it is the closest available test and it does not support the entry.
6. Exact falsifiable prediction: let D_t be the cross-sectional dispersion of trailing 30-day returns across the point-in-time universe (for example the cross-sectional standard deviation, or the interquartile range, of trailing 30-day returns), computed from data known at t. Condition dates into deciles of D_t computed from trailing history only. Prediction: the winner-minus-loser cross-sectional momentum spread over the next 1 month (top decile of trailing 30-day return minus bottom decile, equal-weighted, same point-in-time universe) is significantly *smaller* in the top decile of D_t than in the bottom decile; the effect is nonlinear and concentrated in the upper tail of dispersion, so the middle deciles should show little difference; and the effect must remain after controlling for (i) Bitcoin realized volatility over the same window and (ii) the average pairwise cross-asset correlation across the universe. Falsification: the momentum spread is equal or larger in the high-dispersion regime; or the difference disappears once Bitcoin realized volatility and average cross-asset correlation are controlled; or the effect is present only in a single sub-period or only in one era. **Amendment (v1.1): the prediction is unchanged; what is added is the prior-art position of the claim and the answer to the entry's open question about testability.** (a) The registered prediction stands as written, and the falsification-handling rule is not engaged because no test of it has failed. (b) The closest prior test of this entry's exact mechanism exists and was killed. A preregistered test in the public martex-quant ledger conditioned the cross-sectional momentum spread on the cross-sectional dispersion of trailing 30-day returns - the same state variable, the same book, a different universe, deciles replaced by terciles and a 7-day forward window replaced by a 1-month one - and it was killed: the spread was monotone *increasing* across dispersion terciles (+0.50%, +1.05%, +1.40%) with a top-minus-bottom difference of +0.90% and a 95% block-bootstrap interval of [-2.66%, +3.69%]. The observed direction is the opposite of this entry's registered prediction, which says the spread should be *smaller* in the high-dispersion regime. That test is not decisive - it is wide, it used a hindsight universe, and it used terciles on a daily state variable rather than deciles on a trailing one - but it is the closest available test and it does not support the entry. (c) A second preregistered sibling - a breadth dial conditioning the same momentum book on the share of the universe with positive trailing returns - was also killed on the same pattern (monotone +0.90%, +1.15%, +2.92%; top-minus-bottom +2.02%, CI [-2.10%, +5.74%]). The family 'a cross-sectional state variable conditions the cross-sectional momentum spread' now has two killed preregistered tests, one of which leans against the entry and one of which is merely null. (d) The base effect's own measurement was audited preregisteredly in the same ledger: re-running an unchanged momentum-rotation specification on a point-in-time universe rather than one selected at the end of the sample retained 58% of Sharpe (1.47 to 0.86) and 49% of CAGR (+42.91% to +21.06%), cleared neither a Sharpe of 1.0 nor a deflated-Sharpe bar of 0.95, and reversed the best year (2026: +92.80% to -18.61%). The momentum base this entry switches is therefore materially weaker on an honest universe than on a hindsight one, and the entry's own published source had already warned that its own inferences are sensitive to dynamic universe construction. (e) The free-data question is answered in the seventh cycle and the answer is negative for this entry's essential input in the regime it tests; see item 10.
7. Candidate explanatory variables and proposed signal concept: signal = a *state variable* (cross-sectional dispersion of trailing 30-day returns) used to switch or scale a momentum exposure, not itself a directional signal. Plain language: "when the coins are pulling in opposite directions, the winners-and-losers ranking has stopped working." Timing: D_t is computed from returns up to and including t; both the momentum ranking and the forward window start after t. Universe membership at t must be determined using only information available at t (listing age, market-cap and volume screens computed on trailing data only).
8. Expected trade expression: cross-sectional within the universe: long the top trailing-return decile and short the bottom, with exposure scaled by the dispersion state (full weight at low dispersion, zero or negative at high dispersion) — equivalently, the dispersion state as a filter on an otherwise unconditional momentum book. Shorting feasibility is a separate question (borrow availability, funding paid on the short leg, venue rules, margin). The expression is a risk-budgeting switch on a known base effect, not a standalone directional bet.
9. Primary comparator or null explanation: (i) unconditional cross-sectional momentum on the identical universe and dates — the mandatory incremental bar; (ii) the same momentum book scaled by a *Bitcoin-realized-volatility* regime variable instead of dispersion (the generic-market-state null); (iii) the same momentum book scaled by the average pairwise cross-asset correlation (the redundancy null); (iv) momentum conditioned on trailing market-cap or liquidity (a size/quality null); (v) a time-shuffled dispersion series as a placebo; (vi) a random-assignment placebo in which the dispersion state is permuted across dates, preserving its distribution but destroying its timing. Falsification criteria are controls (ii) and (iii) reproducing the effect, which would mean dispersion adds nothing beyond generic market state.
10. Data required: (a) point-in-time universe membership — listing date, contemporaneous market capitalisation, and quote volume for each token; (b) daily spot close and quote volume per token per venue. **Ninth-cycle construction (the blocker is removed):** the point-in-time universe is now buildable on free data *without* historical market capitalisation, using the Binance public data archive (https://data.binance.vision, access 1, no key). Under data/futures/um/daily/klines/ there is a dated daily kline file per symbol per calendar day for every USD-M perpetual symbol ever listed - 998 distinct symbols are present, including contracts the venue has since delisted. The construction is: *listing date* = the first daily file with a non-zero trade count; *last trading date* = the last daily file with a non-zero trade count, because a ceased contract's final file carries volume=0, trade count=0 and a frozen close (and, as the tenth cycle established, the file series may also simply *end* at the delisting date - see the tenth-cycle correction below); and *universe membership at date t* = symbols whose trading interval contains t. Two hazards are recorded and must be handled: the archive's monthly tree is incomplete for some symbols (ANTUSDT monthly ends 2023-05 while its daily series continues to 2024-05-28; CVCUSDT monthly ends 2024-09 while its daily series continues to 2026-09), so the daily tree must be used; and a symbol can be re-listed (CVCUSDT is verified zero-volume through 2024 and trading again on 2026-09-15), so lifetimes must be reconstructed as segments rather than one interval per symbol. The construction was validated against two independently announced venue delistings: LUNAUSDT's last file with real trading is 2022-05-12 (volume 70.7bn, 20.1m trades) against the venue's 2022-05-13 delisting, and OMGUSDT trades through 2025-01-20 and is zero-volume from 2025-02-10, immediately after the venue's announced 2025-01-31 settlement (first announced 2024-12-06 and twice postponed). **Tenth-cycle correction to this construction (the rule survives; the ninth cycle's *description* of it did not).** Direct checks against the archive on 2026-10-20 produced four findings that every validator of this entry must now carry. (i) **The kline tree has an interval subdirectory**: the path is `data/futures/um/daily/klines/{SYMBOL}/1d/{SYMBOL}-1d-{DATE}.zip`, and a construction that omits the `1d` component fetches nothing at all - the ninth cycle's notes recorded only that the `daily/metrics/` tree has no interval subdirectory, which is true and easy to over-generalise. (ii) **There are two different terminal shapes, and the ninth cycle's description fits only one of them.** For a *delisted* contract the file series simply **ends** at the delisting date: LUNAUSDT has 13 daily files in May 2022 and none in any month after, and the final file (2022-05-13) is zero-filled (close 0.0080 frozen, volume 0, trades 0) against the previous file (2022-05-12: volume 70,710,754,248, trades 20,104,720), so the statement that "every later file is zero-volume" is **wrong for the delisted case** - there are no later files - although the derived answer (last real trade 2022-05-12) is unchanged and was re-verified by direct read. For a contract that is *inactive but not delisted* the series **continues indefinitely** with zero-volume files: OMGUSDT has files for 2025-02, 2025-08 and 2026-01, and 2026-01-15 reads volume 0, trades 0, close 0.2910, against the last real trade on 2025-01-20 (volume 15,588,667.6, trades 71,050). The rule "**last trading date = the last date with a non-zero trade count**" is therefore robust to both shapes and to a missing terminal file, but the **terminal state must be recorded per symbol** (zero-filled tail versus series-ended) and never assumed, and a zero-volume tail is a suspension-or-delisting ambiguity resolved by the data cutoff rather than by the archive. (iii) **The archive is versioned data and must be treated as such.** Its README publishes an exhaustive, dated update log of retroactive file replacements, and the single kline event listed (2022-08-08, "Fixed inconsistent data") enumerates **73,234 replaced files across 1,659 spot symbols** with original and replacement SHA-256 checksums, while every archive file also ships a `.CHECKSUM` beside it. Retroactive correction is therefore real and not bounded by the venue's good intentions, but it is enumerable and auditable: a validator must read the update log, apply the listed replacements, verify each file against its checksum, and record the archive revision date alongside the results. The 2022-08-08 event is spot-only, so the futures kline tree used by this construction was not touched by it, but the rule binds every future run. (iv) **The free screens must be stated as rules, not as intentions**, because the point-in-time universe is the binding input: minimum 90 days of trading history before a symbol is eligible, a trailing 30-day quote-volume rank, and a fixed universe size reselected on a fixed schedule - the same construction the closest preregistered prior art uses - so that a free replication is comparable to the literature it is testing rather than to a different universe. A parallel spot tree exists (data/spot/monthly/klines/, BTCUSDT monthly from 2017-08) with the same lifetime logic, so a spot implementation is equally constructible. **What the construction does not fix:** the published result's *market-capitalisation* screen still has no free history (CoinGecko's own API reference documents that the free Demo tier is restricted to the past 365 days, the paid Basic tier to two years and only Analyst and above to the full range; CoinPaprika's free tier refuses daily historical tickers before the trailing 365 days; CoinLore is current-only; DefiLlama's per-token historical supply and vesting endpoints require a Pro key; CoinMarketCap's keyless tier is current-only with historical listings paywalled), so a free replication must substitute listing-age and trailing-volume screens and is therefore a replication rather than an exact replication. Free sources (checked 2026-10-20): CoinGecko public API `/api/v3/coins/markets` (keyless public tier with rate limits and a documented cache; documentation at https://docs.coingecko.com/reference/coins-markets — exposes market cap, market-cap rank, total volume, circulating supply, fully diluted valuation and price-change windows; **access 2** — the keyless tier returns the *current* universe and current market caps, so a point-in-time universe must be reconstructed from a historical snapshot source, and that reconstruction is not confirmed feasible on free data); Binance public data archive https://data.binance.vision (monthly and daily klines and trades for spot pairs, including symbols subsequently delisted — **access 1** for prices); OKX public candle history and Bybit V5 kline endpoints (**access 1** for prices). Known limitations: survivorship — a token delisted after a collapse must remain in the universe with mark-to-next-delisting treatment, otherwise the high-dispersion regime is precisely the regime in which the sample is thinned; free historical market-cap snapshots are the binding constraint; low-liquidity tokens have stale closes and inflated return variance; venue fragmentation means the universe must be defined per venue or on one benchmark venue with an explicit choice. **Free-data determination (v1.1, verified by direct fetch on 2026-10-20; this supersedes the v1.0 access-2 note on the universe and answers the entry's own open question).** (a) The CoinGecko public API *rejects historical data requests from the free tier*. GET https://api.coingecko.com/api/v3/coins/bitcoin/market_chart?vs_currency=usd&days=max and GET /coins/bitcoin/market_chart/range over a window longer than 365 days both return HTTP 401 with error_code 10012 and the message 'Your request exceeds the allowed time range. Public API users are limited to querying historical data within the past 365 days. Upgrade to a paid plan to enjoy full historical data access'. The keyless endpoint that does work is GET /coins/markets, which returns only *current* market cap, market-cap rank, total volume, circulating supply, total supply, max supply and fully diluted valuation. **Consequence: a contemporaneous market-cap or market-cap-rank screen back to 2019 is not constructible from CoinGecko on free data.** (b) CoinLore's free API (https://api.coinlore.net/api/ticker/?id=90 and /api/coin/markets/?id=90) returns current market cap and current supply only; its historical chart endpoint returns HTTP 404. (c) The CoinCap free API was not reachable without a key in this cycle's check, so it is not confirmed as a free route. (d) What *is* confirmed free: the Binance public data archive (https://data.binance.vision) contains daily and monthly spot klines for pairs that have since been delisted, so a *listing date* and a *trailing quote-volume* screen are constructible on free data - which is exactly the construction used in the public preregistered point-in-time universe audit, and which is why that audit could remove the hindsight-inclusion half of the bias. (e) What is **not** constructible on free data: the *delisting* half of survivorship. Binance's live API lists only pairs active today, so a token that was genuinely a top-volume name in 2019 and has since been delisted cannot enter a free point-in-time universe; the same audit recorded that four of its 40 symbols had been delisted within six weeks of its snapshot date, that two of them were among the five worst performers in the live window, and that its own result is therefore an *upper bound* on the honest Sharpe. Free historical market-cap snapshots that would allow a market-cap-based substitute are not available either, per (a) to (c). (f) Why this is decisive for this entry rather than merely inconvenient: the entry's registered prediction is conditioned on the *high* cross-sectional dispersion state, and high dispersion is exactly the state in which launches, unlocks, parabolic moves and delistings concentrate, so the residual survivorship bias is largest precisely in the regime the hypothesis tests - the same mechanism the cycle-6 rejection of the stablecoin-supply proxy and the cycle-6 deferral of on-chain exchange reserves both turned on.
**Thirteenth-cycle additions to the data determination (2026-10-20), which sharpen an existing record rather than change it.** (a) **A public executable replication of this entry's own construction exists**, published as a strategy listing (a QuantConnect terminal cache and the corresponding Quantpedia strategy page, both access 1), and it specifies the construction in operational detail: a 20-day cumulative log return, cross-sectionally demeaned and scaled to unit gross exposure; the state variables computed from information available through t-1; the dispersion overlay written as `exposure = min{1, max(0.10, median(dispersion history) / current_dispersion)}` with a recursive smoothing filter of lambda = 0.80, bounds [0.10, 1.00] and a one-day lag; daily rebalancing; and Bitcoin realized volatility over 30 days and average off-diagonal correlation over 60 days as the rival state variables. *Establishes* that the entry's construction is public prior art in executable form, and that the rival-state-variable comparison the entry demands has already been performed by a third party in the same construction. *Does not establish* any independent test of the entry's registered prediction; it is a replication of the entry's own working paper, not a third-party test of the state-dependence claim. (b) **That replication depends on a paid data tier**, and the dependency is on precisely the input this catalogue has already recorded as unobtainable for free: the published construction takes "CoinGecko Pro for the full cryptocurrency side of the dataset: prices, market capitalizations, and trading-activity screens", i.e. the $1bn market-capitalisation screen and the trading-activity screen are both paid-tier inputs, and CoinGecko's free tier refuses every historical query (HTTP 401, error_code 10012, 365-day limit). *Establishes* that the entry's construction as published is **not** reproducible on free data, and therefore that a free replication is a **replication, not an exact replication** — which sharpens, and does not change, the existing free-data score of 3/5. (c) **A practitioner recap of the entry's own working paper is public** (a 28 April 2026 weekly research recap, access 1, marketing-adjacent and used here only as evidence that the idea has been publicised rather than as evidence of any result), reporting the entry's headline numbers as an improvement in Sharpe and a reduction in maximum drawdown. *Establishes* that the hypothesis is already public, promoted and priced in the practitioner channel; *does not establish* anything about its validity.
11. Plausible friction and capacity considerations: cross-sectional crypto trades pay taker fees on both legs, spread and slippage on the short leg, funding on the short leg of a perpetual implementation, and margin on two sides; with a monthly formation window turnover is not dominant, but the short leg's borrow and the tail risk of a crowded long book are the real costs; capacity in the smallest tokens is poor, so a liquidity screen is mandatory; because the base momentum effect's realised variance is heavy-tailed, drawdown control dominates any expected-value estimate. No friction values are invented.
12. Why meaningfully different from the catalogue and prior art: it is the catalogue's only non-derivatives entry and its only cross-sectional breadth mechanism. CRYPTO-EDGE-001, -002 and -003 are all single-instrument derivatives positioning measures acting on a market-wide factor; this entry measures *disagreement across assets* and acts on the *ranking* of assets rather than on the direction of the market. It is not a renamed moving-average crossover, not a single-asset volatility filter, and not another indicator added to a list. Prior-art scoping: the effect has already been published — Zhang and Makgolo (2026), "Cross-Sectional Dispersion and the State Dependence of Cryptocurrency Momentum" — which is why the originality score is held to 3/5 and why the entry is scoped as a *retail-testable, free-data, survivorship-aware specification* of a published state-dependence rather than as a new claim. The marginal contribution claimed here is (a) a preregisterable free-data test design for a published effect, and (b) the explicit heavy-tail inference requirement that the momentum-factor literature requires, which the published work does not address for a tradable specification. Novelty is not claimed globally, and the scope of the literature search is recorded in SOURCE_NOTES.md.
13. Strongest argument against; likely failure regimes; confounders: (i) **the base effect may not exist in a tradeable form** — Grobys and Sapkota (2019) find no momentum in crypto for 2014-2018, and Grobys and Shahzad (2026) show that the realised variances of six crypto momentum strategies follow power laws so that the population mean and variance are statistically not defined under block bootstrap, concluding that variance-based performance metrics are uninformative; a conditional overlay on a base effect whose mean may be unestimable is a weak proposition. (ii) **the incremental content may be nil** — the published claim is that dispersion adds information beyond Bitcoin realized volatility and average cross-asset correlation; if the crypto momentum spread is already dominated by generic market state, dispersion is a noisy proxy for it. (iii) **universe construction drives the answer** — the published work states that inference is sensitive to dynamic universe construction, and a free-data implementation is more exposed to survivorship than the original. (iv) **heavy tails cut both ways** — in the high-dispersion regime the momentum spread's left tail is fattest, so the mean can fall even where the median does not, which makes a mean-based test the wrong instrument and can make a real effect look like a null. (v) **confounding with launches and unlocks** — a high-dispersion state is often produced by a small number of new or newly issued tokens entering the universe, so the state variable may be a proxy for "the universe changed" rather than for disagreement. (vi) **crowding** — the momentum book is the most crowded cross-sectional book in crypto, so the high-dispersion regime is also when it is most likely to be unwound together, which is an argument for the overlay and a confound for its attribution. Likely failure regimes: low-volatility, high-momentum periods where the overlay would suppress the base effect; a universe whose membership churns; any window where the top-decile winners are driven by a single idiosyncratic token. (vii, new in v1.1) The base effect is materially smaller than the published state's own framing implies. A preregistered audit of an unchanged momentum-rotation specification found that roughly 40% of its measured Sharpe and half of its CAGR came from being allowed to rank coins that were in the universe only because of what happened later, with the degradation concentrated in the incumbent's biggest years and one year reversing sign; the honest Sharpe was at most 0.86 and 'plausibly lower', and the residual was stated to be an upper bound because delisted coins cannot enter. The entry's own published source had separately warned that inference about cryptocurrency momentum is sensitive to dynamic universe construction, which is precisely the variable that determines the answer. (viii, new in v1.1) The entry's incremental claim is now contradicted in direction by the closest available test: the dispersion dial was killed with a point estimate leaning the opposite way, and the breadth-dial sibling was killed on the same pattern; the family's two preregistered tests are both null or adverse. (ix, new in v1.1) The free-data standard cannot be met for the essential input in the tested regime, and the reason is structural rather than a matter of finding the right source: the delisting half of survivorship is not observable on free data, and the market-cap screen that would be the natural substitute is not available in history either.
**Thirteenth-cycle addition, and it is adverse to the entry's base rather than to its registered claim: the base effect is now documented in a peer-reviewed source as close to non-existent.** Han, Kang and Ryu, "Momentum in the Cryptocurrency Market: A Comprehensive Analysis under Realistic Assumptions", *Review of Asset Pricing Studies*, published 13 October 2026, doi 10.1093/rapstu/raag011 — the published abstract states that prior studies "overlook liquidation risk and other real-world frictions and inadequately assess its profitability", that when portfolios are marked to market daily **many cross-sectional momentum portfolios are liquidated**, that those with statistically significant mean returns **often earn negative profits**, that "the mean return is insufficient for testing profitability in the cryptocurrency market, where returns are heavily skewed and fat-tailed", and that "**evidence of time-series momentum is strong, whereas evidence of cross-sectional momentum is almost nonexistent**". This is *stronger* adverse evidence on the base than the working-paper version the catalogue recorded in the eleventh cycle, and it corroborates the eleventh cycle's non-move (the refusal to raise this entry's robustness to 2/5 on a favourable survivorship-aware source about a different universe): the base has since been documented as near-absent in a peer-reviewed source, so the favourable source cannot be a reason to raise robustness. It does **not** test the entry's registered claim, which is about *state dependence* of the momentum spread rather than the existence of the spread, so the falsification-handling rule is not engaged and the entry is not downgraded for a failed test.
**A second, new confounder: size composition.** A 2026 study of the size-momentum structure in crypto (Xu and Wu; encountered in this cycle only through a practitioner summary, and the underlying paper's venue and sample were **not** verified, so it is recorded as a lead rather than as evidence) reports that the smallest crypto quintile shows a weekly *reversal* spread of 4.9% while the largest quintile shows +1.0% weekly momentum, with the effect lengthening at longer horizons. If that is right, the sign of a cross-sectional momentum spread is a function of the universe's size composition, and the entry's published construction screens on a $1bn market capitalisation — which is not incidental but *constitutive*. The free replication's substituted screen (listing age and trailing volume, in place of market capitalisation) selects on a **different** axis, so the free replication may not merely be noisier than the published one; it may be estimating a *different* quantity. This is a second, independent reason why a free replication of this entry is a replication and not an exact replication, and it should be stated in any plan for the entry.
**A third new objection, cost-side and specific to this entry's expression.** The entry's own prior sits on the retail side of the forced unwind: front-end traders are 96.0% of three-group liquidation volume and bear $344 million of forced losses against $118 million returned by auto-deleveraging, and the market in which a selection book must exit is one whose depth is 66-79% of pre-event and still contracting. A daily-rebalanced cross-sectional momentum book is a *selection* book with a daily clock, which is the structure the catalogue's select-versus-harvest prior identifies as the exposed case; and the two independent 2026 sources that bear on the cost side of exactly that structure now say so quantitatively — a peer-reviewed source finds that many cross-sectional momentum portfolios are liquidated and that those with statistically significant mean returns often earn negative profits, and an independent 15-minute study on 183 Binance pairs finds the gross reversal edge peaks near 1.3 basis points per trade against a 5 basis-point round-trip cost, "large enough to detect, too small to clear benchmark spot capture costs". The entry's net-value score of 1/5 is therefore now *evidenced* rather than merely inferred.
14. Research-only validation plan to be executed elsewhere: **Cycle-10 specification (v1.3, complete — preregister before inspection):**
- **Universe rule (binding):** use the Binance public data archive (`https://data.binance.vision`) USDⓈ-M perpetual **daily** klines at the `1d` interval (`data/futures/um/daily/klines/{SYMBOL}/1d/`); the interval subdirectory is mandatory and a construction that omits it fetches nothing. *Listing date* = first daily file with non-zero trade count. *Last trading date* = last daily file with non-zero trade count. **The terminal state is recorded per symbol and is never assumed**: a delisted contract's series *ends* at the delisting date (LUNAUSDT: files end 2022-05-13 and the final file is zero-filled), while an inactive-but-not-delisted contract's series *continues* with zero-volume files (OMGUSDT: files still present in 2026-01). The rule is robust to both shapes and to a missing terminal file, but the two are different facts and are logged separately. Trading lifetimes must be reconstructed as **segments** (re-listings occur; e.g. CVCUSDT). Do **not** use the archive's monthly tree for lifetime questions (e.g. ANTUSDT monthly ends 2023-05 while daily continues to 2024-05-28; CVCUSDT monthly ends 2024-09 while daily continues to 2026-09). Universe membership at date t requires the trading interval to contain t. **Amended in v1.6 (fourteenth cycle — one addition, made in advance of any interpretation).** **The size composition of the free universe must be declared, and the base-effect sign must be established on the same population before the switch is tested.** The thirteenth cycle recorded, as a hypothetical, that "if the momentum sign differs by size quintile, then the free screen (age and volume) and the published screen (market capitalisation) select different populations with different signs". The fourteenth cycle's verification of the size-momentum source and its two corroborations make that condition *documented rather than hypothetical*, so the specification now requires: (a) the validator **declares the free universe's size composition** before the switch is estimated, using whatever free proxy of size is available (contemporaneous market capitalisation is not free and dated — the CoinGecko chart endpoint returns HTTP 404 and the free tier refuses historical queries with HTTP 401 — so the declaration must state that the size proxy used is listing age and trailing volume, or a current market-cap snapshot used only as a coarse strata label, and must say which); (b) the validator **estimates the unconditional momentum spread on the same population** and reports its sign and magnitude before the conditional spread, because a negative unconditional spread in a size stratum makes the entry's registered comparison — *the spread is smaller in high dispersion than in low* — uninterpretable as a test of the switch rather than of the base sign; and (c) the validator runs a **size-matched control**: the same momentum spread computed within size strata (for example within the largest available stratum of the free screen, and within the smallest), so that the conditional comparison is made *within* a size composition rather than across two. A null under the free screen without (b) and (c) is therefore **not** evidence for or against the entry's registered prediction, and the validator must say so in the write-up rather than reporting it as a failed test. Everything else in the cycle-10 specification is unchanged, and no empirical validation was performed in this repository.
- **Archive versioning rule (binding, new in the tenth cycle):** the archive is versioned data. Before any run, read the archive's Updates changelog (https://github.com/binance/binance-public-data, "Updates"), apply the enumerated replacements (the 2022-08-08 kline event replaced 73,234 files across 1,659 spot symbols with old and new checksums), verify every file against its `.CHECKSUM`, and record the archive revision date alongside the results.
- **Free screens (binding, stated as rules):** because historical market capitalisation is not free, substitute **listing-age and trailing-volume screens**: a symbol becomes eligible only after 90 days of trading history; the universe is the top 40 by trailing 30-day quote volume, reselected every 90 days; a symbol leaves the universe when its trailing volume rank falls out of the top 60. This is a replication, not an exact replication of the published market-cap screen, and it is deliberately the same construction the closest preregistered prior art uses so that the free replication is comparable to the literature it tests. Mark delistings at last traded price and carry positions to a preregistered terminal value, and **report the headline result both including and excluding delisted tokens**, because the sign of the delisting bias on a book that is short the losers is not established anywhere and is therefore not a one-sided correction.
- **New-listing contamination control (new in the tenth cycle):** the state variable must not be mechanically produced by newly listed symbols entering the universe - the closest preregistered prior art warns in terms that a point-in-time selector admits recently listed contracts whose funding swings far more than a major's, which would raise measured results in the least trustworthy way. Enforce the 90-day minimum history inside the state computation, recompute the state variable on a fixed-membership sub-universe, and report the state variable with and without the youngest cohort.
- **Momentum specification:** trailing 30-day return ranking, top-minus-bottom decile, equal-weighted, 1-month forward window, non-overlapping.
- **Dispersion/state variable:** cross-sectional standard deviation (primary) of trailing 30-day returns over the point-in-time universe; state variable D_t in trailing percentiles computed from trailing history only; nonlinearity tested in the upper tail (deciles). **Rival state variable (mandatory, new in the tenth cycle):** average pairwise cross-asset correlation over the same window, run through the identical statistic. Peer-reviewed work on regime dependence in crypto cross-sectional signals treats a market-wide regime as the operative variable, and if the correlation state variable carries the same or more signal then the dispersion switch is not the operative variable and the entry's claim must be rewritten accordingly.
- **Primary statistic (mandatory):** **difference of spreads** between high- and low-dispersion states (spread_high - spread_low), not the level of either spread. The null outcome "base spread does not survive costs so there is nothing to switch on" must be an available outcome.
- **Controls (mandatory):** (i) Bitcoin realized volatility over the same window, now a *documented competing driver* rather than a nuisance control, because peer-reviewed work finds cross-sectional crypto signal reliability to swing with a market-wide volatility regime; (ii) average pairwise cross-asset correlation across the universe, reported as both a control and a rival state variable; (iii) liquidity-filter control; (iv) the new-listing contamination control above.
- **Incremental bar:** the difference of spreads must be larger than implied by unconditional momentum, must survive controls (i)-(iv), must exceed what the Bitcoin-volatility-scaled momentum variant delivers, and must remain positive when the rival correlation state variable is substituted.
- **Inference (heavy-tail):** block bootstrap with the cross-section kept intact within blocks, median-based summaries, report **volatility of the signal bucket** alongside the mean difference, and **no Sharpe ratio** (Grobys & Shahzad 2026).
- **Costs:** report gross and net of taker fees, slippage on both legs, funding on short leg (perp implementation), and borrow where spot used.
- **Out-of-sample/robustness:** time-based split with thresholds/rules fixed in training; leave-one-asset-out, leave-one-year, pre-2022 vs post-2022; regime coverage (trending bull/bear, high-volatility, token-launch-heavy), with the token-launch-heavy cell mandatory rather than descriptive because it is where the new-listing contamination is most likely to bind.
- **Falsification:** no difference of spreads between states; difference vanishes after the volatility or correlation control; the rival correlation state variable carries the whole effect; effect only in a single sub-period; depends on the exclusion of 2-3 largest contributors; base effect non-estimable/rejected; or difference of spreads is null once the base spread is known cost-negative.
Do not execute this plan in this repository. **Amended in v1.4 (eleventh cycle - four amendments to the cycle-10 specification, all forced by direct verification of the constructions the specification describes, all made in advance of any data inspection):** (a) **The listing-pagination rule is corrected and becomes binding.** When listing the archive's kline or metrics tree, use **no** delimiter and page by supplying `marker=<the last key returned>`; the response in this mode carries **no** continuation token, and the response in delimiter mode carries `<NextMarker>`. A validator must assert that the number of daily files obtained for a symbol is consistent with the listing's own pagination state before using any terminal date, and must assert that a symbol's last daily file is not earlier than the most recent date on which the symbol traded on any other reference (for example the announcement feed). This is a hard precondition, not advice: on 20 of 20 sampled symbols the naive first-page listing returns a mid-life date. (b) **The terminal-state rule is restated and a third state is added.** The terminal trading date of a contract is the last date on which its daily kline carries a **non-zero trade count**; the file series may (i) end at that date, (ii) continue with zero-volume, zero-trade files and a frozen close, or (iii) do either of those things for a contract that was never delisted. The state is a property of the venue's file emission and is **not** a function of delisting status; the tenth cycle's two-state taxonomy (delisted ends, inactive continues) is **superseded in place** because it is refuted by the six contract instances checked on 2026-10-20, in each of which a delisted contract's series continued. A validator must record the terminal state per symbol from the file contents, and must not infer the state from whether the contract was delisted. (c) **The universe must be cross-checked against an independent reference.** The venue's dated delisting-announcement feed (CMS catalog id 161) is an independent record of settlement dates and contract symbols. The universe's membership dates are therefore built from the archive's trade counts and *validated* against the announcement feed wherever the two overlap, with every disagreement reported. A symbol that the archive says ended before its announced settlement, or continued to trade after it, is a construction error and must be reported, not silently reconciled. (d) **The archive-versioning rule is narrowed.** The archive's `.CHECKSUM` files are verified as working (SHA-256, byte-for-byte verified on one futures kline file on 2026-10-20), and the replaced-file enumerations are downloadable - but they live in the **GitHub repository's** `updates/` directory, the README's "Updates" table lists only two of the three published update artefacts, and the single kline event covers the **spot** tree. The rule is therefore: read the README's Updates table, read the `updates/` directory listing on GitHub directly rather than relying on the table alone, apply the enumerated replacements, verify every file's `.CHECKSUM`, and **record the versioning status of the futures tree as unestablished** rather than assuming the spot rule transfers. The v1.3 specification items are otherwise unchanged. Do not execute this plan in this repository.
15. Evidence notes with sources and dates: **Tenth-cycle sources (2026-10-20).** Publisher **Eleventh-cycle sources (2026-10-20).** Binance CMS delisting-announcement feed (access 1, no key; `.../cms/article/catalog/list/query?catalogId=161&pageNo=N&pageSize=20` and `.../cms/article/detail/query?articleCode=<code>`, the latter returning `publishDate` as epoch milliseconds) - 442 articles over 23 pages back to 2022-02-24, of which 76 are perpetual-contract delisting notices; *establishes* a dated, machine-readable venue record of perpetual delistings; *does not establish* any return effect. Binance S3 archive listing API, response shape verified directly (access 1) - *establishes* that in the no-delimiter mode the response contains no continuation token and that pagination requires supplying `marker=<last key returned>`, and that 20 of 20 sampled symbols are truncated by a naive first-page listing; *does not establish* anything about returns. Binance archive 1d kline files, six contract instances across four delisting notices read directly (access 1) - *establishes* that the last non-zero-trade date equals the announced settlement date in every instance, and that delisted contracts' file series continue with zero volume, zero trades and a frozen close; *does not establish* anything about returns, and no performance statistic was computed. Binance archive `.CHECKSUM` file, SHA-256 verified against a downloaded futures kline zip (access 1) - *establishes* that per-file checksum verification is implementable as specified; *does not establish* anything about the completeness of the update log. binance-public-data repository README and `updates/` directory listing (access 1) - *establishes* that the README's Updates table lists two of the three published update artefacts and that the enumerations are hosted in the repository rather than in the S3 bucket; *does not establish* that the futures tree is versioned. "Cryptocurrency as an Investable Asset Class: Coming of Age", arXiv:2510.14435v4 (access 1) - *establishes* that a survivorship-aware universe of 18,622 live coins plus 29,230 inactive or delisted coins with historical data, screened on market capitalisation, supports crypto-size, crypto-momentum and crypto-value smart beta that remains robust, and that perpetual futures account for more than 98% of Bitcoin futures volume in the Schmeling, Schrimpf and Todorov sample; *does not establish* anything about the dispersion state variable, and it is a preprint whose universe, screen and asset class differ from this entry's, so it is favourable evidence about the *base* effect rather than about the entry's registered prediction. "Crashing Together, Rallying Apart: Dynamic Conditional Tail Dependence in Cryptocurrency Markets", arXiv:2606.16840 (access 1) - *establishes* that the conditional tail-dependence structure of the thirteen largest cryptocurrencies is time-varying over 89 overlapping windows from late 2021 to 2025, with a high-dependence regime through 2022, a trough around mid-2024 following the spot-ETF approval, a recovery into the late-2024/2025 rally, and a tail-dominance flip whose date the authors leave informal; *does not establish* anything about cross-sectional momentum reliability, but it establishes that any cross-asset co-movement state variable is **non-stationary**, which is a competing-explanation input for this entry's state-variable choice. Han, Chulwoo, Kang, Byeongguk and Ryu, Jaehyeon, "Time Series and Cross Sectional Momentum in the Cryptocurrency Market: A Comprehensive Analysis under Realistic Assumptions" (2024, access 1) - *establishes* that on a survivorship-aware sample including inactive cryptocurrencies, cross-sectional momentum evidence is weak while time-series momentum evidence is strong, and that many momentum portfolios are liquidated once daily price fluctuations and transaction costs are accounted for; *does not establish* anything about this entry's state variable, and it is directly adverse to the base effect's tradability. **No dimension moves on the strength of these sources**: the new survivorship-aware momentum evidence bears on the base effect rather than on the entry's registered prediction, and the entry's registered prediction is still untested and still has no published test. **Tenth-cycle sources (2026-10-20).** Publisher record: "Regime-Dependent Predictability of Cryptocurrency Distress: Cross-Sectional Evidence from Two Exchanges", Journal of Risk and Financial Management 19(8):599, published 7 August 2026, doi 10.3390/jrfm19080599, open access (the MDPI article page returned HTTP 403 to a direct fetch, so the abstract was verified through the Crossref record - access 1 for the metadata and abstract, access 2 for the full text) - on a coin-quarter panel of 609 USD-quoted coins traded on Kraken between 2013 and 2025, built survivorship-inclusively and replicated on Binance, the paper finds that the onset of severe sustained distress is predicted by realized volatility, illiquidity, weak momentum and asset youth (coin-stratified cross-validated out-of-sample AUC about 0.68); that this predictability is *regime-dependent* on a market-wide volatility regime (jointly significant interactions, likelihood-ratio p < 0.001, with prospective accuracy swinging from AUC 0.43 to 0.79 across years - informative in calm markets and unreliable in turbulent ones); that this regime-dependence is robust across alternative distress thresholds, regime proxies, data frequencies, cluster-bootstrap inference and replication on a second exchange, though the individual signal channels are not; and that the most natural mechanism - rising cross-asset co-movement in turbulent markets - finds **no support**; *establishes* that the reliability of cross-sectional crypto signals is documented to depend on a market-wide regime, which makes this entry's dispersion state variable one candidate among at least two and turns the entry's volatility control into a documented competing driver rather than a nuisance check, and separately weakens any mechanism in which a co-movement or dispersion-compression channel is assumed to be the operative one; *does not establish* anything about the entry's registered prediction, which concerns *momentum* reliability rather than distress prediction, and it is not a replication of the entry's source. "State transitions and momentum effect in cryptocurrency market", Finance Research Letters (2025), doi 10.1016/j.frl.2025.108356, peer-reviewed, access 2 (publisher paywall; the abstract and highlights are free and were read through the search index and the publisher's own abstract page) - using a quantile-panel persistence method that accounts for cross-sectional dependence, the paper finds that crypto momentum arises only in UP-UP market-state transitions and is absent in DOWN-DOWN and transitional regimes, with a weaker but similar pattern in meme stocks; *establishes* independent, peer-reviewed evidence that crypto momentum is state-dependent, which supports this entry's *premise* while making the field more crowded and the choice of state variable contested, since this paper's state variable (market-state transitions) is a different one from the entry's (cross-sectional dispersion); *does not establish* anything about the entry's registered prediction, and its momentum measure is a long-only rather than a long-short spread, so the comparison is not exact. Binance public data archive README and Updates changelog (https://github.com/binance/binance-public-data, access 1, fetched directly 2026-10-20) - see item 10: the kline tree's interval subdirectory, the two distinct terminal shapes (series-ended for a delisted contract, zero-filled tail for an inactive one), the exhaustive dated update log whose single kline event (2022-08-08) replaced 73,234 files across 1,659 spot symbols with old and new checksums, and the per-file `.CHECKSUM` files that make the archive auditable; *establishes* that the entry's construction is auditable and versioned rather than merely available; *does not establish* anything about the prediction. **No dimension moves on the strength of these sources**: they bear on the premise, on the competing explanation and on the construction, not on the registered prediction, and the entry's evidence quality (2/5), robustness (1/5) and net value after frictions (1/5) are already at or near their floors, so no re-score is warranted. **Ninth-cycle sources (2026-10-20).** Arefev, "Cross-Sectional Momentum in Cryptocurrency: A Net-of-Costs Replication on Tradable Binance Perpetuals (2020-2026)", SSRN 7404139 (access 2 - the SSRN abstract page returned HTTP 403 to a direct fetch, so the abstract was read via the search index) - an independent replication of cross-sectional momentum on a universe of instruments directly tradable by retail participants on Binance USD-M perpetual futures, using 832 archived perpetual symbols, a daily-close panel, pre-committed J/K parameters, explicit survivorship handling and three significance tests with different dependence assumptions; neither of the two literature-backed specifications permitted under the pre-committed protocol (J=1/K=1 and J=2/K=2) yields a net-of-costs spread distinguishable from zero under any of the three tests; the gross J=1/K=1 spread of +0.573% per week is of the same order as realistic costs of 0.40pp per week and disappears into noise once they are subtracted; and the J=2/K=2 net spread additionally flips sign with an arbitrary choice of rebalancing phase; *establishes* independent, survivorship-aware, cost-aware evidence that the base cross-sectional momentum spread does not translate into a tradable retail strategy on a perpetual universe in 2020-2026, and a precedent for the survivorship-aware perp-universe construction; *does not establish* anything about this entry's registered state-dependence claim. Binance public data archive, https://data.binance.vision (access 1, fetched directly 2026-10-20) - 998 distinct USD-M perpetual symbols in the daily kline tree including delisted contracts, with terminal files for ceased contracts carrying volume=0 and trade count=0 and a frozen close, giving an exact listing date and last trading date per symbol and therefore a survivorship-aware point-in-time universe for free; *establishes* that the entry's essential input is constructible; *does not establish* anything about returns, and no performance statistic was computed from these files in this repository. CoinPaprika public API (access 1 for asset metadata, 3 for history) - GET /v1/coins returns 62,305 assets, 50,436 flagged is_active=false, with a genuine first-seen date per asset, but its historical-ticker endpoint refuses daily history before the trailing 365 days and its last_data_at field tracks the aggregator's last observation rather than a cessation date, so it is a first-seen and active-flag source, not a delisting-date source. Original source list continues: Zhang, Cong and Makgolo, Olorato MacDonald, "Cross-Sectional Dispersion and the State Dependence of Cryptocurrency Momentum" (written 25 April 2026, last revised 27 April 2026), SSRN 6648082, abstract freely readable via the SSRN abstract page and search index; the SSRN PDF delivery URL returned HTTP 403 to automated fetch on 2026-10-20, so **access 2**. Provides: a dynamic, survivorship-aware universe reconstructed from the contemporaneous CoinGecko top 500 with rolling screens for age, market capitalisation, trading activity and return quality; the finding that momentum remains economically meaningful but that *lagged cross-sectional dispersion*, not generic market volatility, is the variable most closely associated with subsequent momentum breakdown, established in horse-race regressions, orthogonalization tests and asset-level panel specifications controlling for Bitcoin realized volatility and average cross-asset correlation; that the effect is nonlinear and concentrated in the upper tail of dispersion, consistent with deterioration in the reliability of the ranking signal; that dispersion-based scaling improves full-sample drawdown and certainty-equivalent outcomes while Bitcoin-volatility scaling performs better in post-2020 long-only implementation exercises; and that inference about cryptocurrency momentum is sensitive to dynamic universe construction. Establishes: a published, methodologically explicit state-dependence of crypto momentum on cross-sectional dispersion. Does not establish: a net-of-costs tradable payoff, a forward-return claim at the individual-token level, or an independent replication — the working paper is not peer-reviewed and no replication was found in this cycle's searches. Grobys, Klaus and Shahzad, Syed Jawad Hussain, "Cryptocurrency Momentum: Is It an Illusion?", International Journal of Finance and Economics 31(2):2180-2193 (April 2026), doi 10.1002/ijfe.70036, abstract freely readable via the publisher abstract page and the RePEc/IDEAS index, access 2; provides: the realised variances of six cryptocurrency momentum strategies follow power laws; the population mean and variance of the momentum factor realised variances are statistically not defined under block bootstrap; the performance metrics evaluating the profitability of crypto momentum strategies that use variance as an input are not informative; and there is cross-sectional dependence in the tail risk of momentum strategies built on different formation periods; establishes the inference constraint used in items 11, 13 and 14, and is the strongest published objection to any momentum-based entry; does not address dispersion conditioning. Grobys, Klaus, Kolari, James W., Sandretto, Davide, Shahzad and Aijo (2025), "Cryptocurrency momentum has (not) its moments", Financial Markets and Portfolio Management 39:443-476, free PDF at https://link.springer.com/content/pdf/10.1007/s11408-025-00474-9.pdf, access 1 for the PDF; provides evidence that crypto momentum performance is state-dependent and suffers poor periods analogous to the equity momentum experience, constructed on a 30 large-cap equal-weighted universe; establishes context and a further reason the momentum base effect needs a regime qualifier; establishes no dispersion-specific result. Grobys, Klaus and Sapkota, Niranjan (2019), "Cryptocurrencies and momentum", Economics Letters 180:6-10, abstract freely readable via the IDEAS/RePEc index, access 2; provides the contrary finding of no momentum in crypto over 2014-2018; establishes that the base effect is era-dependent; establishes no dispersion-specific result. Fracassi, Cesare and Kogan, Shimon, "Pure Momentum in Cryptocurrency Markets" (2022), free PDF, access 1; provides the behavioural mechanism for the momentum base effect — investors respond positively to changes in *reported* returns unrelated to any information release, so salience-driven buying generates pure momentum, with a long-short implementation on the two lowest and two highest 24-hour lagged returns reported at an annualised 538% and a Sharpe of 3.52 before costs; establishes a documented underreaction-to-salience mechanism and, by extension, a reason to expect momentum reliability to depend on how salient returns are; the reported pre-cost figures are not relied on as evidence of an edge. CoinGecko public API documentation, https://docs.coingecko.com/reference/coins-markets, checked 2026-10-20: access 2 for universe construction (the keyless public tier exposes current market caps and ranks with rate limits and a documented cache, not point-in-time history) and access 1 for current market-cap, volume and supply fields. Binance public data archive https://data.binance.vision, checked 2026-10-20: access 1 for spot klines including symbols subsequently delisted. Labels: published findings (the dispersion state-dependence, the power-law variance constraint, the momentum era-dependence, the salience mechanism) are separated from unverified assumptions (the dispersion definition, the 30-day formation window, the decile thresholds, the claim that a free-data point-in-time universe can be reconstructed, and the incremental value over Bitcoin realized volatility in a free replication). **Added in v1.1 (all checked 2026-10-20):** martex-quant public research ledger, 'Hypothesis 71 - Point-in-Time Universe: is the rotation edge hindsight?' (docs/hypotheses/71-point-in-time-universe.md, verdict 2026-08-28), free GitHub raw, access 1 for the document; a preregistered test of the *measuring instrument* rather than of an edge, with the pool collected first and two declared cells (point-in-time reselection every 90 days and every 365 days) and no other parameter searched; pool 469 collected and 393 usable, common window 2,880 days from 2018-08-17 to 2026-07-05; the hindsight-universe arm reproduces the published figures exactly (CAGR +42.91%, Sharpe 1.47, MDD -29.01%), and the point-in-time 90-day cell returns CAGR +21.06%, Sharpe 0.86, MDD -33.23% with DSR 0.2759, so 58% of Sharpe and 49% of CAGR are retained and Gate A (Sharpe >= 1.0, DSR >= 0.95) and Gate B (Sharpe >= 70% of the hindsight figure) both fail; the 365-day low-churn cell is *worse* (Sharpe 0.73), so the gap is not a turnover artifact; the overlap with the hindsight 40 was 20-28% in 2018-2020 and rose monotonically to 55% by 2025; four of the 40 symbols had been delisted from Binance within six weeks of the snapshot date, two of them among the five worst live performers, and the result is explicitly an upper bound; establishes that a survivorship-aware point-in-time crypto universe is *partially* constructible on free data - the hindsight-inclusion half is removable and the delisting half is not - and that doing so removes roughly 40% of a cross-sectional momentum book's measured Sharpe; establishes no dispersion-specific result. martex-quant 'Hypothesis 38 - Dispersion dial' (docs/hypotheses/33-40-timeseries-batch.md, verdict 2026-07-12), free GitHub raw, access 1 for the document; dispersion defined as the cross-sectional standard deviation of trailing 30-day returns across rankable coins each day, with the top-2-minus-bottom-2 forward 7-day momentum spread compared between high- and low-dispersion terciles, two-sided, on the wide 40-coin daily panel over 64,484 symbol-days; verdict KILLED - the spread was monotone across terciles (+0.50%, +1.05%, +1.40%) but the top-minus-bottom difference of +0.90% had a 95% block-bootstrap interval of [-2.66%, +3.69%]; establishes the closest available preregistered test of this entry's mechanism, killed, with a point estimate whose direction is the opposite of the entry's registered prediction; establishes no conclusion about the entry, because the test used a hindsight universe, terciles on a daily state variable and a 7-day forward window. martex-quant 'Hypothesis 37 - Breadth dial' (same document and verdict), free GitHub raw, access 1 for the document; breadth defined as the share of rankable coins with a positive trailing 90-day return, with the same momentum book compared across breadth terciles; verdict KILLED - monotone (+0.90%, +1.15%, +2.92%) with a top-minus-bottom difference of +2.02% and a 95% interval of [-2.10%, +5.74%];
